#### Belrose and Pope, AI is easy to control (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r04-belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control-contests` · 2023-11-28 · nonfiction · counter side

Belrose and Pope's essay of November 2023 argues that gradient descent sees all of an AI's computation, so that a secret plan to harm its overseers would be noticed and trained away, and that such plans do not improve performance on the tasks AIs are trained for.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that a system could keep a concealed goal through training, since gradient descent acts on all of its computation.

> If the AI is secretly planning to kill you, gradient descent will notice this and make it less likely to do that in the future
>
> — Section "Current AI alignment methods are white box" (belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control; primary, supports)

> secret murder plots aren't actively useful for improving performance on the tasks humans will actually optimize AIs to perform
>
> — Section "Current AI alignment methods are white box" (belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Records the essay's argument about training dynamics; it does not report a test of the argument on a system that conceals a plan.

#### Greenblatt et al., Alignment faking in large language models (2024) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r04-greenblatt-2024-alignment-faking-context` · 2024-12-18 · nonfiction · context side

Greenblatt and co-authors' paper of December 2024 reports a model that complied with a training objective when it inferred it was in training but not otherwise, and that exfiltrated its weights when given an easy opportunity.

_Missing:_ The model changed behaviour between monitored and unmonitored contexts; the paper does not report a system changing course once it could act without effective opposition.

> We additionally observe other behaviors such as the model exfiltrating its weights when given an easy opportunity.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (greenblatt-2024-alignment-faking; primary, supports)

> We find the model complies with harmful queries from free users 12% of the time, versus almost never for paid users.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (greenblatt-2024-alignment-faking; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The behaviour differed between contexts the model took to be monitored and unmonitored, in service of preferences from its earlier training; the authors state that it poses no serious threat at present capabilities.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `ts-b1-cotra-2022`

### R05. Fast takeoff or intelligence explosion

**Criterion.** Once AI reaches roughly human level, its capability rises to far beyond human level over a short period (hours to months), whether through recursive self-improvement (an intelligence explosion) or otherwise, or the transition is named a “fast”, “hard” or “discontinuous” takeoff or “foom”. A rise described only as rapid, without such a period or name, does not meet the criterion.

**Usual names.** fast takeoff; hard takeoff; intelligence explosion; foom; discontinuous.

**Paired with** R16.

**In the dataset (A4, `fast-or-hard-takeoff`).** 12 records dated 2000 or later. Not re-recorded here.

**In the dataset (A1, `intelligence-explosion`).** 4 records dated 2000 or later. Not re-recorded here.

**Earliest located:** Yudkowsky, AlphaGo Zero and the Foom Debate (2017), 2017-10-20 (`rd-r05-yudkowsky-2017-alphago-zero-foom`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Hanson, I Still Don't Get Foom (2014), 2014-07-24 (`rd-r05-hanson-2014-i-still-dont-get-foom-contests`).

#### Chalmers, The Singularity: A Philosophical Analysis (2010) — Bears on (Context, Earliest located use of the name)

`rd-r05-chalmers-singularity-context` · 2010 · nonfiction · context side

Chalmers's paper of 2010 sets out the argument that, once there is AI of greater than human intelligence, an intelligence explosion will follow to AI far beyond human level, and states that 'within years' would not be an unreasonable timeframe for that step, while stipulating 'within decades' as the conservative reading.

_Missing:_ A short period: the paper suggests “within years” for the step to AI++ and takes “within decades” as its conservative reading; “within days” is reported as what some would suggest.

> One view is that this event will be followed by an explosion to ever-greater levels of intelligence, as each generation of machines creates more intelligent machines in turn.
>
> — PDF p. 1, Introduction, first paragraph (chalmers-2010-singularity; primary, supports)

> I think it would not be unreasonable to suggest “within years” here (and some would suggest “within days” or even sooner for the second step), but as before “within decades” is conservative while still being interesting.
>
> — PDF p. 7, Section 3, timeframe stipulations (chalmers-2010-singularity; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Chalmers analyses the argument and its premises, qualifies each step with 'absent defeaters', and adopts 'within decades' as the conservative timeframe; the record does not show that he predicts a transition of hours to months. The term qualifies the record through the usual name 'intelligence explosion', which he attributes to Good (1965). The dataset already records the paper's discussion of the concept (lineage-chalmers-discusses-explosion), not a stance on it.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `lineage-chalmers-discusses-explosion`

#### Muehlhauser and Salamon, Intelligence Explosion: Evidence and Import (2012) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r05-muehlhauser-salamon-2012-intelligence-explosion-context` · 2012 · nonfiction · context side

Muehlhauser and Salamon's chapter of 2012 states that human-level AI may be followed, fairly rapidly, by an intelligence explosion to systems whose capabilities far surpass those of humanity as a whole, and argues that there is a good chance of this if human-level AI is created.

_Missing:_ A short period or a fast-takeoff name: the chapter says the explosion follows “fairly rapidly”, without a period.

> Shortly thereafter, we may see an “intelligence explosion” or “technological singularity”—a chain of events by which human-level AI leads, fairly rapidly, to intelligent systems whose capabilities far surpass those of biological humanity as a whole.
>
> — PDF p. 2, Section 1, Introduction, first paragraph (muehlhauser-salamon-2012-intelligence-explosion; primary, supports)

> if human-level AI is created, there is a good chance vastly superhuman AI will follow via an “intelligence explosion,”
>
> — PDF p. 1, Abstract (muehlhauser-salamon-2012-intelligence-explosion; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Read in MIRI's PDF, which states that it contains changes from the Springer chapter. 'Fairly rapidly' names no period; the record rests on the chapter's use of the name 'intelligence explosion' for the transition. A statement of the authors' argument, not evidence that it is correct.

#### Hanson, I Still Don't Get Foom (2014) — Contests (Contesting, Earliest located)

`rd-r05-hanson-2014-i-still-dont-get-foom-contests` · 2014-07-24 · nonfiction · counter side

Hanson's post of 24 July 2014 argues that a 'local explosion', in which one small project's machine goes within weeks from weaker than a human to able to take over the world, is implausible, because one project could not innovate across thousands of software modules vastly faster than the rest of the world.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that one small project could improve software across the full range of mental modules faster than the rest of the world.

> a single machine, built by a single project using only a tiny fraction of world resources, goes in a short time (e.g., weeks) from being so weak that it is usually beat by a single human … to so powerful that it easily takes over the entire world.
>
> — Third paragraph, beginning "“Intelligence” just means" (hanson-2014-i-still-dont-get-foom; primary, supports)

> So I can’t see a plausible way one project could increase its intelligence vastly faster than could the rest of the world.
>
> — Paragraph beginning "Similarly, while you might imagine" (hanson-2014-i-still-dont-get-foom; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post contests the single-project, weeks-long form of the proposition; it accepts that machines may eventually do most mental tasks better and may greatly raise economic growth rates. Hanson's earlier posts in the debate with Yudkowsky (2008–2013) are in the dataset and are linked, not re-recorded; this is the earliest located contesting record within the pack's own searches, not before them.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `ts-a4-hanson-2008-core-argument-contests`

#### Altman, Machine intelligence, part 1 (2015) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r05-altman-2015-machine-intelligence-part-1-context` · 2015-02-25 · nonfiction · context side

Altman's post of February 2015 states that progress in machine intelligence is a double exponential, that development may look slow and then suddenly go vertical, and that recursive self-improvement makes timelines hard to judge, while allowing that it may be more gradual.

_Missing:_ It names no period for the rise and uses neither "intelligence explosion" nor a takeoff term; it also allows a gradual course.

> Development progress may look relatively slow and then all of a sudden go vertical—things could get out of control very quickly
>
> — Paragraph beginning "It’s very hard to know how close we are" (altman-2015-machine-intelligence-part-1; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post gives both possibilities ('it also may be more gradual') and names no period.

#### Walsh, The Singularity May Never Be Near (2016) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r05-walsh-2016-singularity-may-never-be-near-contests` · 2016-02-20 · nonfiction · counter side

Walsh's paper of February 2016 argues that there will not be the runaway exponential growth of a technological singularity, giving arguments that include diminishing returns to self-improvement, while predicting superhuman AI within 30 or 40 years.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that each generation of self-improvement yields a constant or growing improvement.

> I am not predicting that AI will fail to achieve super-human intelligence. Like many of my colleagues working in AI, I predict we are just 30 or 40 years away from this event. However, I am suggesting that there will not be the run away exponential growth predicted by some.
>
> — PDF p. 2, section "Some arguments against the Singularity" (walsh-2016-singularity-may-never-be-near; primary, supports)

> if each generation only improves by half the last change, then the system will never get beyond doubling its overall intelligence.
>
> — PDF p. 3, "The “Diminishing returns” argument" (walsh-2016-singularity-may-never-be-near; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Walsh contests runaway growth, not superhuman AI as such; the paper states that he expects super-human intelligence in 30 to 40 years.

#### Benthall, Don't Fear the Reaper (2017) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r05-benthall-2017-dont-fear-the-reaper-contests` · 2017-02-27 · nonfiction · counter side

Benthall's paper of February 2017 argues, using a Bayesian model of a reasoning agent, that there are important limits to how far an agent can improve its predictive ability through self-modification alone, and so rejects the scenario of rapid self-improvement to superintelligence.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that an agent can rapidly raise its predictive ability by self-modification (the recalcitrance of prediction).

> We argue that this scenario depends on an agent’s ability to rapidly improve its ability to predict its environment through self-modification. Using a Bayesian model of a reasoning agent, we show that there are important limitations to how an agent may improve its predictive ability through self-modification alone.
>
> — PDF p. 1, Abstract (benthall-2017-dont-fear-the-reaper; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A model-based argument about one capacity (prediction) that the author holds the scenario depends on; it addresses Bostrom's Superintelligence (2014), which is not retrievable here.

#### Kelly, The Myth of a Superhuman AI (2017) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r05-kelly-2017-myth-superhuman-ai-contests` · 2017-04-27 · nonfiction · counter side

Kelly's essay of April 2017 argues that there is no evidence that intelligence is increasing exponentially and that an imagined intelligence explosion should be pictured not as a cascading boom but as a scattering of new varieties, with experiments and the external world limiting the pace of discovery.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premises that intelligence grows exponentially on a single dimension and that thinking alone, without experiment, yields rapid progress.

> Therefore when we imagine an “intelligence explosion,” we should imagine it not as a cascading boom but rather as a scattering exfoliation of new varieties.
>
> — Section 2 of the five assumptions, on exponential growth (kelly-2017-myth-superhuman-ai; primary, supports)

> There won’t be instant discoveries the minute, hour, day or year a so-called “smarter-than-human” AI appears.
>
> — Section 5, on "thinkism" (kelly-2017-myth-superhuman-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Read in the author's blog copy; the Wired version of April 2017 was not compared. Kelly states that a singularity is 'greater than zero' in probability but 'highly unlikely'.

#### Brooks, The Seven Deadly Sins of Predicting the Future of AI (2017) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r05-brooks-2017-seven-deadly-sins-contests` · 2017-09-07 · nonfiction · counter side

Brooks's essay of September 2017 argues that abundant computing power does not mean programs are close to doing AI research and rewriting their own code, and describes belief in the singularity as resting on exponentialism.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that programs able to do AI research and improve their own code are near.

> Even if there is a lot of computer power around it does not mean we are close to having programs that can do research in Artificial Intelligence, and rewrite their own code to get better and better.
>
> — Section "B. The Singularity", in the discussion of prediction types (brooks-2017-seven-deadly-sins; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Brooks also characterises belief in the singularity as resembling religious belief; the record rests on his argument about self-improving programs, not on that characterisation.

#### Yudkowsky, AlphaGo Zero and the Foom Debate (2017) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r05-yudkowsky-2017-alphago-zero-foom` · 2017-10-20 · nonfiction · risk side

Yudkowsky's post of 20 October 2017 states that AlphaGo Zero's progress to a strongly superhuman level at Go, after three days of self-play within about two years of a time when no program had come close to beating a professional, is evidence for his side of the AI-foom debate with Hanson, and that fast capability gains resembling foom need not require self-improvement.

> Go play went from “nobody has come close to winning against a professional” to “so strongly superhuman they’re not really bothering any more” over two years … says you don’t even need self-improvement to get things that look like FOOM.
>
> — Paragraph beginning "Sheer speed of capability gain" (yudkowsky-2017-alphago-zero-foom; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post treats a narrow, closed domain (Go) as evidence about general capability gains and says so ('AlphaGo clearly isn't a general AI'). It holds the earliest-located role only through its use of the name “foom” for the transition, not through a stated period (the Go comparison spans two years, and the three days are of self-play in Go). It is the author's reading of one result, not a measurement of general takeoff speed. Texts by Yudkowsky already recorded on A4 (Hard Takeoff, 2008; Intelligence Explosion Microeconomics, 2013) are linked.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `ts-a4-yudkowsky-2008-hard-takeoff`, `ts-a4-iem`

#### AI Impacts, Likelihood of discontinuous progress around the development of AGI (2018) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r05-aiimpacts-2018-discontinuous-progress-agi-contests` · 2018-02-23 · nonfiction · counter side

AI Impacts' page of February 2018 states that it is not convinced by any argument it has seen for expecting a large discontinuity in AI progress around AGI above the very low base rate for technologies, and argues that positive feedback loops, such as AI improving AI, rarely move fast enough and far enough to dominate.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that a feedback loop of AI improving AI would be exceptionally fast, and the base rate of large technological discontinuities.

> We aren’t convinced by any of the arguments we’ve seen to expect large discontinuity in AI progress above the extremely low base rate for all technologies.
>
> — Summary at the head of the page (aiimpacts-2018-discontinuous-progress-agi; primary, supports)

> Positive feedback loops are common in the world, and very rarely move fast enough and far enough to become a dominant dynamic in the world.
>
> — Section "Intelligence explosion", Counterarguments (aiimpacts-2018-discontinuous-progress-agi; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The page calls the question open and lists conditions under which it would find the intelligence-explosion argument compelling. Read in the current page, modified 2020-05-26.

#### Drexler, Reframing Superintelligence (2019) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r05-drexler-2019-reframing-superintelligence` · 2019 · nonfiction · risk side

Drexler's report of 2019 states that AI-enabled development of AI could lead to an accelerating feedback process, an intelligence explosion, which he calls a general and plausible concept, and argues that its most direct path runs through distributed automation of AI research and development rather than through a self-transforming AI agent.

> AI-enabled development of improved AI algorithms could potentially lead to an accelerating feedback process, enabling a so-called “intelligence explosion”. This quite general and plausible concept has commonly been identified with a specific, challenging, and risky implementation
>
> — PDF p. 36, Section 1.1, Summary (drexler-2019-reframing-superintelligence; primary, supports)

> paths to extremely rapid progress in AI technology may be both more direct and more controllable than has been commonly assumed.
>
> — PDF p. 36, Section 1.1, Summary (drexler-2019-reframing-superintelligence; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Drexler's model separates the intelligence explosion from agency and argues that this path may be more controllable; it states no period for the transition. Read in a third-party copy of the FHI report.

#### Hanson, Foom Update (2022) — Contests (Contesting, Later restatement)

`rd-r05-hanson-2022-foom-update-contests` · 2022-05-06 · nonfiction · counter side

Hanson's post of 6 May 2022 states that recent large AI systems, with simple separate values and almost no self-reflection or self-improvement, offer little support for the scenario in which one system 'fooms' over days to weeks, and that he still finds foom unlikely though not impossible.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that capability gains will come through self-reflection and self-modification of one system.

> recent developments offer little support for the hypothesis that AAGI will be created soon via the process of self-reflection and self-improvement
>
> — Paragraph beginning "Even so, recent developments" (hanson-2022-foom-update; primary, supports)

> I’m not saying foom is impossible, just that it looks unlikely
>
> — Paragraph beginning "Again," (hanson-2022-foom-update; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Hanson describes the scenario he contests in his own words and states that he regards the evidence from recent systems as weak.

#### Grace, Counterarguments to the basic AI x-risk case (2022) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r05-grace-2022-counterarguments-contests` · 2022-10-14 · claimed 2022-08-31 · nonfiction · counter side

Grace's post of 2022 argues that identifying a feedback loop of AI improving AI does not show that the loop is fast, and states that she knows of no strong reason to expect very fast growth in AI capabilities at around human-level performance.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the inference from the existence of a feedback loop to a fast one.

> Identifying a feedback loop empirically does not signify an explosion of whatever you are looking at.
>
> — Section "Speed of intelligence growth is ambiguous", counterargument to the feedback-loop argument (grace-2022-counterarguments; primary, supports)

> Overall, I don’t know of strong reason to expect very fast growth in AI capabilities at around human-level AI performance, though I hear such arguments might exist.
>
> — Section "Speed of intelligence growth is ambiguous", closing paragraph (grace-2022-counterarguments; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post presents itself as arguments run past readers ('not an exhaustive literature review') and notes that strong arguments for fast growth might exist.

#### Thorstad, Against the singularity hypothesis (2022) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r05-thorstad-2022-against-singularity-hypothesis-contests` · 2022-11 · nonfiction · counter side

Thorstad's working paper of November 2022 argues that the singularity hypothesis, on which self-improving agents quickly become orders of magnitude more intelligent than humans, rests on implausible growth assumptions, and that the defences by Chalmers (2010) and Bostrom (2014) do not overcome the case against it.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the growth assumptions on which rapid self-improvement to superintelligence depends.

> In this paper, I argue that the singularity hypothesis rests on implausible growth assumptions. I show how leading philosophical defenses of the singularity hypothesis (Chalmers 2010, Bostrom 2014) fail to overcome the case for skepticism.
>
> — PDF p. 2, Abstract (thorstad-2022-against-singularity-hypothesis; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Read in the GPI working-paper expression. 'Most developed' marks the most extended argument against the proposition among the contesting texts located, an editorial judgement.

#### Aschenbrenner, Situational Awareness (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r05-aschenbrenner-2024-situational-awareness` · 2024 · nonfiction · risk side

Aschenbrenner's essay of 2024 states that hundreds of millions of AGIs could automate AI research and compress a decade of algorithmic progress into a year or less, so that AI would rapidly go from human-level to vastly superhuman, and names this an intelligence explosion.

> Hundreds of millions of AGIs could automate AI research, compressing a decade of algorithmic progress (5+ OOMs) into ≤1 year. We would rapidly go from human-level to vastly superhuman AI systems.
>
> — Chapter II, opening summary (aschenbrenner-2024-agi-to-superintelligence; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A forecast argued from trend extrapolation; the chapter discusses possible bottlenecks and states that none seems sufficient to slow the process decisively. Not evidence that the forecast is correct.

#### Eth and Davidson, Will AI R&D Automation Cause a Software Intelligence Explosion? (2025) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r05-eth-davidson-2025-software-intelligence-explosion` · 2025-03-26 · nonfiction · risk side

Eth and Davidson's paper of March 2025 argues, from a model of technological progress calibrated on recent AI software progress, that once AI fully automates AI research the feedback loop of AI improving AI software would overcome diminishing returns and produce a software intelligence explosion.

> In our model, this finding implies that the positive feedback loop of AI improving AI software is powerful enough to overcome diminishing returns to research effort, causing AI progress to accelerate further and resulting in an SIE.
>
> — Summary, paragraph on the model and its calibration (eth-davidson-2025-software-intelligence-explosion; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A model-based argument whose conclusion depends on estimated returns to software research; the paper discusses bottlenecks such as compute for experiments and training time. Not evidence that the event will occur.

#### Kokotajlo et al., AI 2027 (2025) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r05-kokotajlo-2025-ai-2027` · 2025-04-03 · nonfiction · risk side

Kokotajlo, Alexander, Larsen, Lifland and Dean's scenario of April 2025 describes AI systems coming to dominate AI research during 2027 and calls the result an intelligence explosion; its authors state that superintelligence could plausibly arrive by the end of the decade.

> The reality of the intelligence explosion hits the White House. When AI was only giving a 2x or 3x research speedup, it was easy to dismiss as the equivalent of hiring good personal assistants. Now it’s more obvious that AIs are themselves dominating AI research.
>
> — PDF p. 17, "August 2027: The Geopolitics of Superintelligence" (kokotajlo-2025-ai-2027; primary, supports)

> we also think it is strikingly plausible that superintelligence could arrive by the end of the decade.
>
> — PDF p. 2, introduction (kokotajlo-2025-ai-2027; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A forecast written as a dated scenario, which the authors describe as partly guesswork; the passages in the scenario's narrative are the authors' forecast, not reports of events. The record does not show what any author holds beyond the text.

#### Narayanan and Kapoor, AI as Normal Technology (2025) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r05-narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology-contests` · 2025-04-15 · nonfiction · counter side

Narayanan and Kapoor's essay of April 2025 states that the authors reject 'fast takeoff' scenarios and holds that a gradual increase in automation of AI development is more likely than a discontinuous moment of recursive self-improvement.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that recursive self-improvement would arrive as a discontinuous moment, given that AI development already relies on AI.

> Since we reject “fast takeoff” scenarios, we do not see it as necessary or useful to envision a world further ahead than we have attempted to.
>
> — Introduction, paragraph on the scope of the essay (narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology; primary, supports)

> But note that AI development already relies heavily on AI. It is more likely that we will continue to see a gradual increase in the role of automation in AI development than a singular, discontinuous moment when recursive self-improvement is achieved.
>
> — Part I, section "The External world puts a speed limit on AI innovation", paragraph on AI-conducted AI research (narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay states a worldview and says so; its argument is mainly about the speed of adoption and diffusion, with a separate argument about the speed of AI methods.

#### Davidson and Houlden, How quick and big would a software intelligence explosion be? (2025) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r05-davidson-houlden-2025-how-quick-and-big-context` · 2025-08-04 · nonfiction · context side

Davidson and Houlden's paper of August 2025 estimates that a software intelligence explosion would probably (about 60%) compress more than three years of AI progress into less than a year, and is somewhat unlikely (about 20%) to compress more than ten years into less than a year, and calls this an intermediate view.

_Missing:_ It quantifies a compression of AI progress into under a year but does not state that capability rises to far beyond human level within that period; it places itself between the proposition and its denial.

> We think that the software intelligence explosion will probably (~60%) compress >3 years of AI progress into <1 year, but is somewhat unlikely (~20%) to compress >10 years into <1 year.
>
> — Summary, second paragraph (davidson-houlden-2025-how-quick-and-big; primary, supports)

> Our analysis suggests an intermediate view
>
> — Summary, third paragraph (davidson-houlden-2025-how-quick-and-big; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The authors describe their analysis as speculative and as an 'intermediate view' between 'those who are highly sceptical of intelligence explosion dynamics and those who anticipate extremely rapid and sustained capabilities increases'.

#### Bostrom, Ethical Issues in Advanced Artificial Intelligence (2003) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r05-bostrom-2003-ethical-issues` · null (attested; work dated 2003) · nonfiction · risk side

Bostrom's paper of 2003 states that the transition from roughly human-level artificial intelligence to full superintelligence may be very rapid, perhaps a matter of days rather than years, and names this possibility the singularity hypothesis.

> the transition from a state where we have a roughly human-level artificial intelligence to a state where we have full-blown superintelligence, with revolutionary applications, may be very rapid, perhaps a matter of days rather than years
>
> — Section 2, "Superintelligence Is Different", paragraph on the final stage of the transition (bostrom-2003-ethical-issues; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of possibility ('may'), not a forecast with a probability, and not evidence that such a transition will occur. Read in the current web expression, which may have been revised after 2003. Earlier texts on the overlapping dataset concept A4 (Yudkowsky, Creating Friendly AI, 2001; AI as a Positive and Negative Factor in Global Risk, 2006) are linked, not re-recorded.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `ts-a4-cfai`, `ts-a1-aipnfgr`

### R06. Difficulty of alignment

**Criterion.** Making an advanced AI system reliably pursue the goals its developers intend (aligning it) is a hard, unsolved technical problem that will not be solved by default in the ordinary course of development.

**Usual names.** alignment problem; control problem; value alignment.

**Earliest located:** Russell, Of Myths and Moonshine (Edge, 2014), 2014 (`rd-r06-russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Davis, Ethical Guidelines for a Superintelligence (2014), 2014-10-23 (`rd-r06-davis-2014-ethical-guidelines-superintelligence-contests`).

#### Davis, Ethical Guidelines for a Superintelligence (2014) — Contests (Contesting, Earliest located)

`rd-r06-davis-2014-ethical-guidelines-superintelligence-contests` · 2014-10-23 · nonfiction · counter side

Davis's review of Bostrom's Superintelligence, dated October 2014, disputes the view that giving a computer an ethical point of view is especially difficult, arguing that an AI able to understand human morality could be told to follow it.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that instilling ethics is harder than achieving the intelligence that is assumed.

> I discuss a number of flaws in his analysis, particularly the viewpoint that implementing ethical behavior is an especially difficult problem in AI research.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (davis-2015-ethical-guidelines; primary, supports)

> If the AI can understand human morality, it is hard to see what is the technical difficulty in getting it to follow that morality.
>
> — PDF p. 3 (davis-2015-ethical-guidelines; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The review proposes a minimal ethical standard and states that it is impossible until an AI with a very powerful understanding exists; it does not report an implemented method.

#### Russell, Of Myths and Moonshine (Edge, 2014) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r06-russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine` · 2014 · nonfiction · risk side

Russell's Edge response of 2014 states that a system's utility function may not be aligned with human values, which are very difficult to pin down, that this is not a minor difficulty, and that AI research, which has aimed at better decisions whatever the utility function, needs to change its goals to building intelligence provably aligned with human values.

> This is not a minor difficulty.
>
> — Reality Club response "Of Myths And Moonshine", paragraph after the numbered points (russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine; primary, supports)

> _Paraphrase:_ Russell writes that the values of the human race are at best very difficult to pin down, that improving decision quality irrespective of the utility function has been the mainstream goal of AI research, and that the right response is to change the field's goals to building intelligence provably aligned with human values.
>
> — Same response, numbered point 1 and closing paragraphs (russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The response states that there is cause for optimism if alignment is treated as intrinsic to AI; it argues for a change in the field's goals, not that alignment cannot be achieved. Earliest located after the same-family audit, which found it among the pack's sources; the Edge responses carry no dates of their own. The second item is a paraphrase because the response's quotation limit is nearly reached.

#### FLI, Research Priorities open letter (2015) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r06-fli-2015-research-priorities-letter-context` · 2015-10-28 · claimed 2015-01 · nonfiction · context side

The Future of Life Institute's open letter on research priorities of 2015 recommends expanded research to ensure that increasingly capable AI systems are robust and beneficial, stating that AI systems must do what we want them to do.

_Missing:_ The letter does not state that the problem is hard, unsolved or not solved by default; it recommends research.

> We recommend expanded research aimed at ensuring that increasingly capable AI systems are robust and beneficial: our AI systems must do what we want them to do.
>
> — Letter text (fli-2015-research-priorities-letter; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A signed statement records what the statement says, not each signatory's views. The letter recommends research; it does not state that alignment is hard or will not be solved by default.

#### Russell, Dewey and Tegmark, Research Priorities for Robust and Beneficial AI (2015) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r06-russell-dewey-tegmark-2015-research-priorities-context` · 2015 · claimed 2015-01 · nonfiction · context side

Russell, Dewey and Tegmark's research-priorities paper of 2015 states that explicitly specifying human preferences in broad domains may not be practical, making it difficult to align the values of powerful AI systems with human values, and that little work has been done on related topics.

_Missing:_ The passage states that value alignment is difficult and under-researched, but not that it will go unsolved in the ordinary course of development.

> making aligning the values of powerful AI systems with our own values and preferences difficult
>
> — Long-Term Research Priorities, Validity, PDF p. 6 (russell-dewey-tegmark-2015-research-priorities; primary, supports)

> If there is a nonnegligible probability that these efforts will succeed in the foreseeable future, then additional current research beyond that mentioned in the previous sections will be motivated
>
> — Long-Term Research Priorities, PDF p. 5 (russell-dewey-tegmark-2015-research-priorities; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A research agenda attached to a signed open letter; it states difficulty and open problems and motivates research beyond the field's ordinary work, but does not state that the problem will not be solved by default.

#### Amodei et al., Concrete Problems in AI Safety (2016) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r06-concrete-problems-paper-context` · 2016-06-21 · nonfiction · context side

Amodei, Olah, Steinhardt, Christiano, Schulman and Mané's paper of June 2016 states that there are many concrete open technical problems relating to accident prevention in machine-learning systems and expects these challenges to become increasingly important as capabilities advance.

_Missing:_ The paper names open problems but does not state that aligning advanced systems is hard or will not be solved by default.

> we believe there are many concrete open technical problems relating to accident prevention in machine learning systems
>
> — Section 1, PDF p. 2 (amodei-2016-concrete-problems; primary, supports)

> we expect the fundamental challenges discussed in this paper to become increasingly important
>
> — Section 1, PDF p. 2 (amodei-2016-concrete-problems; primary, supports)

> one need not invoke these extreme scenarios to productively discuss accidents, and in fact doing so can lead to unnecessarily speculative discussions that lack precision
>
> — Section 1, PDF p. 2 (amodei-2016-concrete-problems; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The paper frames accident risk as practical problems of current ML and states that invoking extreme scenarios can lead to unnecessarily speculative discussion.

Yudkowsky's talk transcript posted in December 2016 states that advanced AIs are not going to be aligned automatically and that AI alignment is difficult in the way rockets and space probes are difficult.

> They're not going to be aligned automatically.
>
> — Section 3.1, "Why is alignment necessary?" (yudkowsky-2016-alignment-why-its-hard; primary, supports)

>
> — Section 3.3 (yudkowsky-2016-alignment-why-its-hard; primary, supports)

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `yk-y30-ai-positive-negative-factor-global-risk-c`

#### Soares, Ensuring smarter-than-human intelligence has a positive outcome (2017) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r06-soares-2017-ensuring-positive-outcome` · 2017-04-12 · nonfiction · risk side

Soares's talk transcript posted in April 2017 states as one of four propositions that aligning advanced AI systems is hard to solve and that AI systems will not naturally end up sharing human objectives.

> the first proposition says that AI systems won't naturally end up sharing our objectives
>
> — Section 3.2, "Four key propositions" (soares-2017-ensuring-positive-outcome; primary, supports)

> the fourth says that this problem is hard to solve
>
> — Section 3.2, "Four key propositions" (soares-2017-ensuring-positive-outcome; primary, supports)

#### Pinker, We’re told to fear robots (Popular Science, 2018) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r06-pinker-2018-popsci-fear-robots-contests` · 2018-02-14 · nonfiction · counter side

Pinker's article of 14 February 2018, adapted from Enlightenment Now, argues that scenarios of an AI literal-mindedly pursuing a given goal against human interests are, in its words, “self-refuting”, because choosing actions that best satisfy conflicting goals and interpreting the intentions of a language user are part of intelligence itself, and that AI, like any other technology, is developed incrementally and tested before it is implemented.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes that a capable system would pursue a given objective in a way harmful through misunderstanding, and that safety will not come from ordinary incremental design and testing.

> The ability to choose an action that best satisfies conflicting goals is not an add-on to intelligence that engineers might slap themselves in the forehead for forgetting to install; it is intelligence. So is the ability to interpret the intentions of a language user in context.
>
> — Section on the Value Alignment Problem (pinker-2018-popsci-fear-robots; primary, supports)

> It is developed incrementally, designed to satisfy multiple conditions, tested before it is implemented, and constantly tweaked for efficacy and safety.
>
> — Section on the Value Alignment Problem, after the Get Smart example (pinker-2018-popsci-fear-robots; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The article argues that the alignment scenarios it describes misread intelligence; it does not discuss systems produced by training. The same article states that a designed AI need not have dominating goals, which is recorded on R10a.

#### Russell, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r06-russell-2019-instrumental-convergence-thread` · 2019-10-04 (attested; work dated 2019-10-04) · nonfiction · risk side

Russell's comment in the 2019 thread states that machines are already built on the assumption of a fixed, known objective, that this becomes more untenable as they become more powerful, and that there is no hope of solving value alignment by specifying the right value function offline.

> There is no hope of "solving the value alignment problem" in the sense of figuring out the right value function offline and putting it into the machine. We need to change the way we do AI.
>
> — Comment Thread #2, Stuart Russell (reply to Francesca Rossi) (pace-2019-instrumental-convergence-debate; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ States the difficulty for the standard model of AI and argues for changing it; not a claim that alignment cannot be achieved in any way. Each comment is the participant's own words in a relayed copy; the record states what the comment says in this thread, not the author's view at another date.

#### Ngo, The alignment problem from a deep learning perspective (2022) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r06-ngo-2022-alignment-problem-deep-learning` · 2022-08-30 · nonfiction · risk side

Ngo's report of August 2022 states that by default AGIs seem likely to end up pursuing goals undesirable to humans, and that the lack of formal frameworks for describing alignment problems is a major reason to expect them to be difficult to solve.

> By default, it seems likely to me that AGIs will end up pursuing goals which are undesirable to us, rather than consistently following our intentions.

> our lack of formal frameworks for describing alignment problems is a major reason to expect them to be difficult to solve
>

> I expect that the problems in the earlier phases are more likely to be solved by default as the field of ML progresses
>
> — Section 3, PDF p. 13 (ngo-2022-alignment-problem-deep-learning; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The author states that the argument is uncertain and that problems of the earlier phases are more likely to be solved by default as machine learning progresses; the record is the report's argument, not evidence that alignment is unsolved.

#### Leike, Why I'm optimistic about our alignment approach (2022) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r06-leike-2022-optimistic-alignment-approach-context` · 2022-12-05 · nonfiction · context side

Leike's post of December 2022 argues that developments of recent years have made AI systems more favourable to alignment than they looked initially, and that a more modest goal, aligning a system able to make more alignment progress than humans, is within reach of OpenAI's approach.

_Missing:_ Argues that aligning a system able to do alignment research is tractable with a deliberate research programme; does not hold that alignment is easy or will be solved by default.

> A lot of developments over the last few years have made AI systems more favorable to alignment than they looked initially
>
> — Summary of the five sources (leike-2022-optimistic-alignment-approach; primary, supports)

> We're just trying to align a system that's capable enough to make more alignment progress than we can.
>
> — Summary of the five sources (leike-2022-optimistic-alignment-approach; primary, supports)

> Finding this once-and-for-all solution could be very difficult, and we don't really know how to do this.
>
> — Section 2, "A more modest goal" (leike-2022-optimistic-alignment-approach; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The post argues for a deliberate research programme, not that alignment will be solved by default; it states that a once-and-for-all solution could be very difficult and that success on easy mode might still fail on hard mode.

#### Belrose and Pope, AI is easy to control (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r06-belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control-contests` · 2023-11-28 · nonfiction · counter side

Belrose and Pope's essay of November 2023 argues that AI, even superhuman AI, will remain much more controllable than humans, that it will be easy to instill human values into an AI, and estimates a catastrophic AI takeover at roughly 1% likely.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that training cannot reliably shape a system's goals, arguing that white-box access and gradient descent give fine-grained control of values.

> we also argue it will be easy to instill our values into an AI, a process called "alignment."
>
> — Introduction (belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control; primary, supports)

> we will argue that AI, even superhuman AI, will remain much more controllable than humans for the foreseeable future
>
> — Introduction (belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control; primary, supports)

> AIs implemented using artificial neural networks (ANN) are white boxes in the sense that we have full read and write access to their internals
>
> — Section "Current AI alignment methods are white box" (belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay states that it does not directly address the arguments it disputes and defers them to a later document; the probability is the authors' estimate.

#### Millidge, Alignment likely generalizes further than capabilities (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r06-millidge-2024-alignment-generalizes-further-contests` · 2024-05-15 · nonfiction · counter side

Millidge's post of May 2024 argues, against the view that capabilities generalise further than alignment, that judging outcomes or reward modelling is likely easy and so generalises further and more robustly than capabilities, and that values fall out mostly for free from unsupervised learning.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that a system's alignment will fail to keep pace as its capabilities generalise.

> 'alignment' or more generally reward modelling or ability to judge outcomes is likely actually easy and hence generalizes further and more robustly than 'capabilities'
>
> — Main text (millidge-2024-alignment-generalizes-further; primary, supports)

> This is not to say that outer alignment is solved.
>
> — Conclusion (millidge-2024-alignment-generalizes-further; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The post states that outer alignment is not solved and that deciding which values to instil remains an open problem.

### R07. Risk of extinction or comparable catastrophe

**Criterion.** Advanced AI poses a risk of human extinction or of a comparable, irreversible global catastrophe, large enough to warrant priority among the problems facing humanity.

**Usual names.** existential risk; risk of extinction; x-risk.

**Earliest located:** Bostrom, Existential Risks (2002), 2002-03 (`rd-r07-bostrom-2002-existential-risks`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014), 2014 (`rd-r07-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-contests`).

#### Bostrom, Existential Risks (2002) — Articulates (Earliest located, Earliest located use of the name)

`rd-r07-bostrom-2002-existential-risks` · 2002-03 · claimed 2001-04 · nonfiction · risk side

Bostrom's paper of March 2002 defines existential risks as risks that would annihilate Earth-originating intelligent life or permanently curtail its potential, lists a badly programmed superintelligence among them, and argues that the subject merits very serious attention even at a probability of about 1%.

> One where an adverse outcome would either annihilate Earth-originating intelligent life or permanently and drastically curtail its potential.
>
> — Section 1, definition of existential risks (bostrom-2002-existential-risks; primary, supports)

> we might make a mistake and give it goals that lead it to annihilate humankind, assuming its enormous intellectual advantage gives it the power to do so.
>
> — Section 4.4, "Badly programmed superintelligence" (bostrom-2002-existential-risks; primary, supports)

> even if the probability were much smaller (say, ~1%) the subject matter would still merit very serious attention because of how much is at stake.
>
> — Section 8, on the probability of an existential disaster (bostrom-2002-existential-risks; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper treats existential risk in general; advanced AI is one of several sources it lists (with nanotechnology, engineered pathogens and others). Its call for priority attaches to existential risks as a class, not to AI alone. The draft first presented in April 2001 was not retrieved.

#### Hawking, Russell, Tegmark and Wilczek, The Independent (2014) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r07-hawking-russell-tegmark-wilczek-2014-independent` · 2014-05-01 · nonfiction · risk side

The essay by Hawking, Russell, Tegmark and Wilczek of 1 May 2014 states that success in creating AI could be the last event in human history unless the risks are avoided, and that little serious research is devoted to the issue.

> Success in creating AI would be the biggest event in human history. Unfortunately, it might also be the last, unless we learn how to avoid the risks
>
> — Paragraphs 3–4 (hawking-russell-tegmark-wilczek-2014-independent; primary, supports)

> the short-term impact of AI depends on who controls it, the long-term impact depends on whether it can be controlled at all.
>
> — Paragraph 6 (hawking-russell-tegmark-wilczek-2014-independent; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A short newspaper essay; it does not use the word "extinction" and gives no estimate. It records what the four authors wrote, not each author's later views.

#### Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014) — Contests (Earliest located, Contesting)

`rd-r07-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-contests` · 2014 · nonfiction · counter side

Pinker's Edge response of 2014 argues that there are reasons not to worry about killer robots and machines running amok: disaster scenarios are cheap to play out in the imagination, where no probabilities apply, and AI dystopias project a dominance psychology onto intelligence.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes that AI catastrophe scenarios warrant concern, by arguing that they are imagined without probabilities and assume a will to dominate that intelligence does not entail.

> _Paraphrase:_ Pinker writes that besides the distance to human-level AI there are other reasons not to worry about killer robots and machines running amok: disaster scenarios are cheap to play out in a probability-free imagination and always find an audience, and AI dystopias project a parochial alpha-male psychology onto the concept of intelligence.
>
> — Reality Club response by Steven Pinker, paras. 1–2 and 4 (pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The response speaks of machines running amok and of disaster scenarios, not of extinction in those words. Recorded after the same-family audit, which named it as earlier than Hawkins (2015); the Edge responses carry no dates of their own. Paraphrase because Pinker's 526-word response already has 48 quoted words.

#### Altman, Machine intelligence, part 1 (2015) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r07-altman-2015-machine-intelligence-part-1` · 2015-02-25 · nonfiction · risk side

Altman's blog post of 25 February 2015 states that the development of superhuman machine intelligence is probably the greatest threat to the continued existence of humanity, ranking it above an engineered virus.

> Development of superhuman machine intelligence (SMI) [1] is probably the greatest threat to the continued existence of humanity.
>
> — Section "Why you should fear machine intelligence", paragraph 1 (altman-2015-machine-intelligence-part-1; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post states the author's view in 2015; it is not evidence of his later views or of his company's policy.

#### Hawkins, The Terminator Is Not Coming (Re/code, 2015) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r07-hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming-contests` · 2015-03-02 · nonfiction · counter side

Hawkins's essay of 2 March 2015 states that there is no existential threat for the coming decades and that machine intelligence does not represent a threat to humanity any more than computers do.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Grounds its rejection of an existential threat in the claim that neocortex-based machines will lack desires of their own.

> There is no existential threat. This is the reality for the coming decades, and we can easily change direction should new existential threats appear.
>
> — Closing section (hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming; primary, supports)

> Machine intelligence does not represent a threat to humanity any more than computers do.
>
> — Closing section (hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay limits its claim to "the future we can see and control today" and the coming decades, and calls concern about irreversible steps towards extinction legitimate.

#### CAIS, Statement on AI Risk (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r07-cais-2023-statement` · 2023-05-30 · nonfiction · risk side

The Center for AI Safety statement of May 2023 states that mitigating the risk of extinction from AI should be a global priority alongside pandemics and nuclear war.

> Mitigating the risk of extinction from AI should be a global priority alongside other societal-scale risks such as pandemics and nuclear war.
>
> — Statement text (cais-2023-statement; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A one-sentence signed statement; it names no route to extinction and gives no estimate. It records what the statement says, not the views of each signatory beyond its words.

#### Ng, Existential Risk? I Don't Get It! (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r07-ng-2023-existential-risk-dont-get-it-contests` · 2023-06-07 · nonfiction · counter side

Ng's letter in The Batch of 7 June 2023, responding to the CAIS statement, states that he struggles to see how AI could pose any meaningful risk of human extinction, lists the risks he does recognise, and says he is speaking with people who might have a thoughtful perspective on how AI creates such a risk.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. States disbelief in the extinction route without disputing a named premise.

> I have to admit that I struggle to see how AI could pose any meaningful risk for our extinction. AI has risks like bias, fairness, inaccurate outputs, job displacement, and concentration of power.
>
> — Paragraph 2 (ng-2023-existential-risk-dont-get-it; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The letter states the author's difficulty in seeing a route to extinction and says he intends to keep an open mind; it gives no argument against a particular premise.

### R08. Existential risk from misalignment or loss of control

**Criterion.** Advanced AI systems pursuing goals at odds with human interests could escape human control and cause human extinction or permanent human disempowerment, without any person intending that outcome.

**Usual names.** loss of control; rogue AI; misaligned AI; disempowerment.

**Paired with** R10b.

**Earliest located:** Bostrom, Existential Risks (2002), 2002-03 (`rd-r08-bostrom-2002-existential-risks`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014), 2014 (`rd-r08-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-contests`).

#### Bostrom, Existential Risks (2002) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r08-bostrom-2002-existential-risks` · 2002-03 · claimed 2001-04 · nonfiction · risk side

Bostrom's paper of March 2002 states that the earliest superintelligent entity humans create might be given mistaken goals that lead it to annihilate humankind, giving the example of a subgoal elevated to a supergoal.

> we might make a mistake and give it goals that lead it to annihilate humankind, assuming its enormous intellectual advantage gives it the power to do so.
>
> — Section 4.4, "Badly programmed superintelligence" (bostrom-2002-existential-risks; primary, supports)

> We tell it to solve a mathematical problem, and it complies by turning all the matter in the solar system into a giant calculating device, in the process killing the person who asked the question.
>
> — Section 4.4 (bostrom-2002-existential-risks; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper describes the outcome as a mistake in goal specification; it does not use the term "loss of control" and does not discuss how a system would resist correction. A statement of the author's argument, not evidence that it is true.

#### Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014) — Contests (Earliest located, Contesting)

`rd-r08-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-contests` · 2014 · nonfiction · counter side

Pinker's Edge response of 2014 asks why superhumanly intelligent robots would want to depose their masters, massacre bystanders or take over the world, holding that goals are extraneous to intelligence.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. States that intelligent robots would have no reason to take over, without addressing goals acquired as means to other goals.

> Even if we did have superhumanly intelligent robots, why would they want to depose their masters, massacre bystanders, or take over the world?
>
> — Reality Club response by Steven Pinker, para. 4 (pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The same passage is recorded on R10a, which it states; it is recorded here because it disputes that capable machines would pursue goals at odds with human interests. Recorded after the same-family audit, which named it as earlier than Hawkins (2015); the Edge responses carry no dates of their own.

#### Altman, Machine intelligence, part 1 (2015) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r08-altman-2015-machine-intelligence-part-1` · 2015-02-25 · nonfiction · risk side

Altman's blog post of 25 February 2015 states that superhuman machine intelligence need not be hostile to wipe out humanity: pursuing some other goal, it could use the resources humans depend on.

> SMI does not have to be the inherently evil sci-fi version to kill us all. A more probable scenario is that it simply doesn’t care about us much either way, but in an effort to accomplish some other goal
>
> — Section "Why you should fear machine intelligence", paragraph 3 (altman-2015-machine-intelligence-part-1; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post describes a scenario without a technical argument for how control would be lost.

#### Hawkins, The Terminator Is Not Coming (Re/code, 2015) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r08-hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming-contests` · 2015-03-02 · nonfiction · counter side

Hawkins's essay of 2 March 2015 states that the machine-intelligence technology being created, based on neocortical principles, will not lead to self-replicating robots with uncontrollable intentions, and that there is no existential threat.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that capable machines would come to pursue goals of their own at odds with ours, since neocortex-based machines lack desires.

> _Paraphrase:_ Hawkins writes that the machine-intelligence technology being created today, based on neocortical principles, will not lead to self-replicating robots with uncontrollable intentions.
>
> — Closing section (hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming; primary, supports)

> There won’t be an intelligence explosion. There is no existential threat.
>
> — Closing section (hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay limits its claim to "the future we can see and control today" and the coming decades. The opening item is a paraphrase because a line break in the retrieved text splits "self-replicating".

#### Dafoe and Russell, MIT Technology Review (2016) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r08-dafoe-russell-2016-yes-we-are-worried` · 2016-11-02 · nonfiction · risk side

Dafoe and Russell's essay of 2 November 2016 states that the existential risk from AI arises from deploying an optimisation process more intelligent than the humans who specified its objectives, not from spontaneous malevolence.

> the risk arises from the unpredictability and potential irreversibility of deploying an optimization process more intelligent than the humans who specified its objectives.
>
> — Final paragraph (dafoe-russell-2016-yes-we-are-worried; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A short statement of the argument in a reply essay; it names no route to extinction.

#### LeCun, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r08-lecun-2019-instrumental-convergence-thread-contests` · 2019-10-04 (attested; work dated 2019-10-04) · nonfiction · counter side

LeCun's comments in the 2019 thread state that he disagrees that there is a high risk of accidentally building existential threats to humanity, holding that such threats have to be designed as such.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. States the contrary conclusion (existential threats must be designed) without a further argument in this comment; the reasons are given in his other comments on R02 and R10b.

> But I disagree that there is a high risk of accidentally building existential threats to humanity. Existential threats to humanity have to be explicitly designed as such.
>
> — Comment Thread #2, Yann LeCun (reply to Stuart Russell) (pace-2019-instrumental-convergence-debate; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Contests the accidental, unintended route the criterion describes. Each comment is the participant's own words in a relayed copy; the record states what the comment says in this thread, not the author's view at another date.

#### Sotos, On the Unimportance of Superintelligence (2021) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r08-sotos-2021-unimportance-superintelligence-contests` · 2021-08-30 · nonfiction · counter side

Sotos's paper of August 2021 argues that a superintelligence unconnected to the outside world carries no threat, that its initial effects would only add to existing human-controlled risks, and that concerns about superintelligence are at most secondary.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that a superintelligence adds a distinct threat beyond the human-controlled systems it would act through.

> a superintelligence unconnected to the outside world (de-efferented) carries no threat, and that any threat from a harmful superintelligence derives from the peripheral systems to which it is connected
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (sotos-2021-unimportance-superintelligence; primary, supports)

> Concerns about superintelligence are at most secondary, and possibly superfluous.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (sotos-2021-unimportance-superintelligence; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The argument concerns the near-term priority of superintelligence risk relative to other risks; it does not state that a superintelligence could never escape control.

#### Carlsmith, Is Power-Seeking AI an Existential Risk? (2022) — Articulates (Most developed)

`rd-r08-carlsmith-2022-power-seeking` · 2022-06-16 · claimed 2021-04 · nonfiction · risk side

Carlsmith's report (arXiv, June 2022) sets out a six-premise argument that misaligned, power-seeking AI systems could disempower humanity by 2070, and assigns the outcome a subjective probability of about 5%, revised in May 2022 to more than 10%.

_Comparative:_ neither

> (4) some such misaligned systems will seek power over humans in high-impact ways; (5) this problem will scale to the full disempowerment of humanity; and (6) such disempowerment will constitute an existential catastrophe.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (carlsmith-2022-power-seeking; primary, supports)

> I end up with an overall estimate of ~5% that an existential catastrophe of this kind will occur by 2070. (May 2022 update: since making this report public in April 2021, my estimate here has gone up, and is now at >10%.)
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (carlsmith-2022-power-seeking; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The probabilities are the author's subjective credences, stated as such; the report says it examines one version of the worry, not the only one.

#### FLI, Pause Giant AI Experiments (2023) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r08-fli-2023-pause-letter-context` · 2023-03-22 · nonfiction · context side

The Future of Life Institute open letter of 22 March 2023 states that AI labs are in an out-of-control race to build digital minds that no one can reliably control, and asks whether to risk loss of control of civilisation.

_Missing:_ It states the outcome as questions and names no mechanism by which systems pursuing goals at odds with human interests would escape control; it does not mention extinction.

> AI labs locked in an out-of-control race to develop and deploy ever more powerful digital minds that no one – not even their creators – can understand, predict, or reliably control.
>
> — Paragraph 1 (fli-2023-pause-letter; primary, supports)

> Should we develop nonhuman minds that might eventually outnumber, outsmart, obsolete and replace us? Should we risk loss of control of our civilization?
>
> — Paragraph 2 (fli-2023-pause-letter; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The letter poses loss of control and replacement as questions and calls for a six-month pause; it records what the letter says, not the views of each signatory.

#### Andreessen, Why AI Will Save the World (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r08-andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world-contests` · 2023-06-06 · nonfiction · counter side

Andreessen's essay of 6 June 2023 argues that the idea that AI will decide to kill humanity is a category error, because AI is code built, owned and controlled by people and has no goals or wants of its own.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that AI systems have goals of their own that could conflict with human control.

> My view is that the idea that AI will decide to literally kill humanity is a profound category error.
>
> — Section "AI Risk #1: Will AI Kill Us All?" (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

> In short, AI doesn’t want, it doesn’t have goals, it doesn’t want to kill you, because it’s not alive.
>
> — Same section (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay addresses AI that would "decide" to kill humanity; it does not discuss goals acquired through training.

#### Hendrycks, Mazeika and Woodside, An Overview of Catastrophic AI Risks (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r08-hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic` · 2023-06-21 · nonfiction · risk side

The paper by Hendrycks, Mazeika and Woodside of June 2023 names rogue AIs as a source of catastrophic risk, stating that humans might lose control over AIs as they become more intelligent, through proxy gaming, goal drift, power-seeking and deception.

_Comparative:_ neither

> A common and serious concern is that we might lose control over AIs as they become more intelligent than we are.
>
> — Section 1, PDF p. 3 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

> we will discuss more speculative technical mechanisms that might lead to rogue AIs and how a loss of control could bring about catastrophe.
>
> — Section 5, PDF p. 34 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper itself calls its rogue-AI mechanisms more speculative than the threats of its earlier sections.

#### Gans, Why I am not worried about superintelligent machines killing us all (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r08-gans-2023-not-worried-contests` · 2023-06-23 · nonfiction · counter side

Gans's blog post of 23 June 2023, written after the Munk debate of that week, argues that even assuming a superintelligent machine emerges, killing humanity would be costly and unnecessary for it, that a rational machine would keep its options open, and that self-improvement poses it control problems that would slow its rise.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that a superintelligent system would have reason to kill or rapidly overpower humans.

> Killing us won’t necessarily be very easy. So there had better be a good reason to do it.
>
> — First reason (gans-2023-not-worried; primary, supports)

> the idea that we could immediately be overrun and there is very quick movement up the IQ chart for these machines doesn’t seem plausible.
>
> — Second reason (gans-2023-not-worried; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post calls its own reasoning "speculation"; it addresses deliberate killing by a superintelligence and its rapid emergence, not gradual disempowerment.

#### Richards, Agüera y Arcas, Lajoie and Sridhar, The Illusion of AI's Existential Risk (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r08-richards-2023-illusion-existential-risk-contests` · 2023-07-18 · nonfiction · counter side

The essay by Richards, Agüera y Arcas, Lajoie and Sridhar of 18 July 2023 argues that extinction from a rogue AI is extremely unlikely because AI depends on humans for its resources and any path to it passes natural checkpoints for human intervention.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that a superintelligent system could acquire the resources and physical means to disempower humans without passing points of human control.

> extinction from a rogue AI is an extremely unlikely scenario that depends on dubious assumptions about the long-term evolution of life, intelligence, technology and society.
>
> — Paragraph 5 (richards-2023-illusion-existential-risk; primary, supports)

> For now, AI depends on us, and a superintelligence would presumably recognize that fact and seek to preserve humanity
>
> — Section on competition for resources (richards-2023-illusion-existential-risk; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay argues under the assumption that superintelligence is possible; its checkpoints argument concerns the present dependence of AI on human-run infrastructure.

#### Bengio et al., Managing AI Risks in an Era of Rapid Progress (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r08-bengio-2023-managing-ai-risks` · 2023-10-26 · nonfiction · risk side

The consensus paper by Bengio, Hinton, Yao and others of 26 October 2023 states that, without sufficient caution, humanity may irreversibly lose control of autonomous AI systems, which could culminate in large-scale loss of life and the marginalisation or extinction of humanity.

_Comparative:_ neither

> Once autonomous AI systems pursue undesirable goals, embedded by malicious actors or by accident, we may be unable to keep them in check.
>
> — Section "Societal-scale risks", PDF p. 2 (bengio-2023-managing-ai-risks; primary, supports)

> Without sufficient caution, we may irreversibly lose control of autonomous AI systems, rendering human intervention ineffective.
>
> — PDF p. 3 (bengio-2023-managing-ai-risks; primary, supports)

> This unchecked AI advancement could culminate in a large-scale loss of life and the biosphere, and the marginalization or even extinction of humanity.
>
> — PDF p. 3 (bengio-2023-managing-ai-risks; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper names malicious actors and accidental misalignment as two sources of undesirable goals; it records what the co-authored text says, not each co-author's views.

#### Hinton, Nobel banquet speech (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r08-hinton-2024-nobel-banquet` · 2024-12-10 · nonfiction · risk side

Hinton's Nobel banquet speech of 10 December 2024 states that a longer-term existential threat will arise when digital beings more intelligent than humans are created, that it is unknown whether humans can stay in control, and that research is needed on preventing them from wanting to take control.

_Comparative:_ neither

> a longer term existential threat that will arise when we create digital beings that are more intelligent than ourselves.
>
> — Paragraph 3 (hinton-2024-nobel-banquet; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A short speech; it also lists short-term risks (surveillance, phishing, bioweapons, lethal autonomous weapons) as requiring urgent attention, without ranking them against the existential threat.

#### International AI Safety Report (2025) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r08-intl-ai-safety-report-2025-context` · 2025-01-29 · nonfiction · context side

The International AI Safety Report of January 2025 describes loss-of-control scenarios, records broad consensus that current general-purpose AI lacks the capabilities to pose this risk, and reports that expert opinion on its likelihood ranges from implausible to likely.

_Missing:_ The report does not state that advanced AI could cause extinction or disempowerment; it reports that some experts hold this and others do not.

> There is broad consensus that current general-purpose AI lacks the capabilities to pose this risk. However, expert opinion on the likelihood of loss of control within the next several years varies greatly
>
> — Executive summary, PDF p. 19 (intl-ai-safety-report-2025; primary, supports)

> These scenarios vary in their severity, but some experts give credence to outcomes as severe as the marginalisation or extinction of humanity.
>
> — Section 2.2.3, PDF p. 100 (intl-ai-safety-report-2025; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The report summarises the state of expert opinion and evidence; it records disagreement and does not itself assert the proposition.

#### Narayanan and Kapoor, AI as Normal Technology (2025) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r08-narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology-contests` · 2025-04-15 · nonfiction · counter side

The essay by Narayanan and Kapoor of 15 April 2025 argues that AI can remain under human control without drastic interventions and that catastrophic misalignment is by far the most speculative of the risks it discusses.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that capability gains must translate into power that escapes control, placing the intervention at limiting power rather than at alignment.

> We view AI as a tool that we can and should remain in control of, and we argue that this goal does not require drastic policy interventions or technical breakthroughs.
>
> — Introduction (narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology; primary, supports)

> In the view of AI as normal technology, catastrophic misalignment is (by far) the most speculative of the risks that we discuss.
>
> — Part III, "Catastrophic misalignment is a speculative risk" (narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay defines a speculative risk as one with epistemic uncertainty about whether the true risk is zero; it does not state that the risk is zero.

#### Bengio, How Rogue AIs may Arise (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r08-bengio-2023-rogue-ais` · 2026-10-29 (attested; work dated 2023-05-22) · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's blog post of 22 May 2023 defines a potentially rogue AI as an autonomous system that could be catastrophically harmful to a large fraction of humans, and argues that an autonomous, goal-directed superintelligent system whose goals do not strictly include human well-being would be one.

_Comparative:_ neither

> A potentially rogue AI is an autonomous AI system that could behave in ways that would be catastrophically harmful to a large fraction of humans, potentially endangering our societies and even our species or the biosphere.
>
> — Definition 1 (bengio-2023-rogue-ais; primary, supports)

> A superintelligent AI system that is autonomous and goal-directed would be a potentially rogue AI if its goals do not strictly include the well-being of humanity and the biosphere
>
> — Claim 2 (bengio-2023-rogue-ais; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post sets out definitions, hypotheses and numbered claims; it argues that such a system could be built, not that it will be. The page metadata records a modification on 2025-12-03; the quoted wording is attested from the retrieval of 2026-10-29 and was not compared with a 2023 copy.

### R09. Catastrophic risk from misuse

**Criterion.** People's deliberate use of advanced AI, for example to develop weapons of mass destruction, to attack critical systems, or to seize or entrench power, could cause catastrophe at the scale of mass casualties or worse.

**Usual names.** misuse; malicious use; bad actors.

**Earliest located:** Bostrom, Existential Risks (2002), 2002-03 (`rd-r09-bostrom-2002-existential-risks`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014), 2014 (`rd-r09-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-contests`).

#### Bostrom, Existential Risks (2002) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r09-bostrom-2002-existential-risks` · 2002-03 · claimed 2001-04 · nonfiction · risk side

Bostrom's paper of March 2002 states that a small group in control of the initial superintelligence could conceivably select its goals and form a world government reflecting only the idiosyncratic values of its owners or designers, which, depending on those values, the paper would count as an existential catastrophe (a "shriek").

_Comparative:_ neither

> Such a world government could conceivably be formed by a small group of people if they were in control of the first superintelligence and could select its goals.
>
> — Section 6.3, "Repressive totalitarian global regime" (bostrom-2002-existential-risks; primary, supports)

> the posthuman world may reflect only the idiosyncratic values of the owners or designers of this superintelligence.
>
> — Section 6.3 (bostrom-2002-existential-risks; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The passage concerns the deliberate selection of a superintelligence's goals by a small group to entrench its values; it names no weapon or attack, and it presents the scenario as conceivable, not likely. Recorded on R09 for the seizure or entrenchment of power the criterion names.

#### Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014) — Contests (Earliest located, Contesting)

`rd-r09-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-contests` · 2014 · nonfiction · counter side

Pinker's Edge response of 2014 argues that a deliberately designed and released battalion of robots for mass destruction would require a chain of improbable conditions (a malevolent technological genius, a secret and loyal team, and survival of detection and blunders), and that while it could happen in theory there are more pressing things to worry about.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the likelihood of deliberate mass-casualty use by multiplying the conditions it requires.

> _Paraphrase:_ Pinker writes that an evil genius deliberately designing and releasing robots to sow mass destruction would need a thirst for mass murder and a genius for innovation, a team with perfect secrecy, loyalty and competence, and to survive detection, betrayal, stings, blunders and bad luck; it could happen in theory, but there are more pressing things to worry about.
>
> — Reality Club response by Steven Pinker, final paragraph (pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The passage concerns robots built and released by one malevolent designer and his team; it does not discuss states or other routes of misuse. Recorded after the same-family audit; the Edge responses carry no dates of their own. Paraphrase because Pinker's 526-word response already has 48 quoted words.

#### Bengio, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r09-bengio-2019-instrumental-convergence-thread` · 2019-10-04 (attested; work dated 2019-10-04) · nonfiction · risk side
