Bengio's comments in the 2019 thread state that even if rogue AI from value misalignment can be mitigated, a single person acting greedily for power could unleash dangerous AIs, and that the misalignment of powerful people or companies with the common good can lead to catastrophic outcomes.

_Comparative:_ neither

> how can we guarantee that no single human will act stupidly (more likely, greedily for their own power) and unleash dangerous AIs in the world? And for this, we don't even need superintelligent AIs, to feel very concerned.
>
> — Comment Thread #2, Yoshua Bengio (pace-2019-instrumental-convergence-debate; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Raised as a further concern beside misalignment, without ranking the two. Each comment is the participant's own words in a relayed copy; the record states what the comment says in this thread, not the author's view at another date.

#### Sotos, On the Unimportance of Superintelligence (2021) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r09-sotos-2021-unimportance-superintelligence` · 2021-08-30 · nonfiction · risk side

Sotos's paper of August 2021 argues that any threat from a superintelligence derives from the peripheral systems it is connected to, that biotechnology combined with humans and narrow "savant" software poses the highest and most imminent existential risk, and that resources should be applied there in preference.

_Comparative:_ misuse-greater

> The analysis concludes that resources should be preferentially applied to mitigating the risk of peripheral systems and savant software.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (sotos-2021-unimportance-superintelligence; primary, supports)

> becoming the software responsible for the highest and most imminent existential risk – ahead of hypothetical risk from superintelligences.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (sotos-2021-unimportance-superintelligence; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper's ranking rests on stated assumptions and a quantitative model of its own; it is the author's argument, not a risk assessment by others. The software it ranks highest is narrow 'savant' software, which it says is not generally superintelligent, and its human-derived risk is a capability to engineer pandemic microbes, without separating deliberate from accidental release.

#### Marcus, AI risk ≠ AGI risk (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r09-marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk` · 2023-03-28 · nonfiction · risk side

Marcus's post of 28 March 2023 states that in the near term he is worried less about superintelligent machines beyond human control than about unreliable but widely deployed AI, that criminals and terrorists using it could contribute to nuclear war or the deliberate spread of pathogens, and that people need to stop worrying only about takeover scenarios and think more about such misuse.

_Comparative:_ misuse-greater

> I am not worried, immediately, about “AGI risk” (the risk of superintelligent machines beyond our control), in the near term I am worried about what I will call “MAI risk”
>
> — Opening section, after the Hinton tweet (marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk; primary, supports)

> Perhaps coupled with mass AI-generated propaganda, LLM-enhanced terrorism could in turn lead to nuclear war, or to the deliberate spread of pathogens worse than covid-19, etc. Many, many people could die
>
> — Paragraph after the Europol excerpt (marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk; primary, supports)

> We need to stop worrying (just) about Skynet and robots taking over the world, and think a lot more about what criminals, including terrorists, might do with LLMs
>
> — Later paragraph (marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The ranking is stated for the near term. The post gives no estimate ("We have no earthly idea") and says its 1% figure was a thought experiment; it also states that future technology may be harder to control. Its "MAI risk" covers unreliability as well as misuse.

#### Hendrycks, Mazeika and Woodside, An Overview of Catastrophic AI Risks (2023) — Articulates (Most developed)

`rd-r09-hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic` · 2023-06-21 · nonfiction · risk side

The paper by Hendrycks, Mazeika and Woodside of June 2023 names malicious use as one of four sources of catastrophic AI risk and states that AI-assisted bioweapons could constitute an existential threat by enlarging the number of people able to create pandemics.

_Comparative:_ neither

> Malicious use. Actors could intentionally harness powerful AIs to cause widespread harm.
>
> — Section 1, PDF p. 3 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

> Engineered pandemics from AI-assisted bioweapons pose a unique challenge, as attackers have an advantage over defenders and could constitute an existential threat to humanity.
>
> — Section 2.1, PDF p. 7 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

> With AI assistants, orders of magnitude more people could have the required skills, thereby increasing the risks by orders of magnitude.
>
> — Section 2.1, PDF p. 8 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper presents scenarios and mechanisms, not estimates; it treats malicious use alongside AI race, organisational risks and rogue AIs without ranking them.

#### Amodei, Senate testimony (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r09-amodei-2023-senate-statement` · 2023-07-25 · nonfiction · risk side

Amodei's oral statement to the Senate Judiciary Subcommittee of 25 July 2023 states that extrapolation from current systems to those expected in two to three years suggests a substantial risk that AI will enable many more actors to carry out large-scale biological attacks.

_Comparative:_ neither

> a straightforward extrapolation of today's systems to those we expect to see in 2 to 3 years suggests a substantial risk that AI systems will be able to fill in all the missing pieces, enabling many more actors to carry out large-scale biological attacks.
>
> — Statement of Dario Amodei (senate-hearing-2023-07-25; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The statement reports the speaker's company's own study, which is not public in the hearing record; it is a statement of the risk as assessed, not evidence of the study's findings. Read in the printed transcript, not in the written testimony.

#### Mouton, Lucas and Guest, RAND red-team study (2024) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r09-mouton-2024-rand-bio-red-team-context` · 2024 · nonfiction · context side

The RAND red-team study by Mouton, Lucas and Guest of 2024 reports no statistically significant difference in the viability of biological attack plans made with or without large language model assistance, and concludes that LLMs as of summer 2023 did not measurably change the operational risk.

_Missing:_ Does not dispute that advanced AI could enable mass-casualty misuse; reports no measurable uplift in biological attack planning from the models tested as of summer 2023 and states that the distance to the capability frontier was not measured.

> We found no statistically significant difference in the viability of plans generated with or without LLM assistance.
>
> — Key findings, PDF p. 1 (mouton-2024-rand-bio-red-team; primary, supports)

> LLMs did not measurably change the operational risk of a biological weapon attack.
>
> — Summary, PDF p. 24 (mouton-2024-rand-bio-red-team; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The finding concerns the models tested (as of summer 2023) and the planning stage only; the report states that it did not measure the distance between the capability frontier and the knowledge needed, and it does not argue that future systems pose no such risk.

#### Bengio, How Rogue AIs may Arise (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r09-bengio-2023-rogue-ais` · 2026-10-29 (attested; work dated 2023-05-22) · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's blog post of 22 May 2023 states that once a recipe for a rogue AI is accessible, one or a few people intending to destroy humanity could build one, and calls this the fastest route to a rogue AI.

_Comparative:_ neither

> if a recipe to obtain a rogue AI is discovered and generally accessible, it is enough that one or a few genocidal humans do what it takes to build one.
>
> — Section on scenarios (bengio-2023-rogue-ais; primary, supports)

> The fastest route to a rogue AI system is if a human with the appropriate technical skills and means intentionally builds it with the objective of destroying humanity or a part of it set explicitly as a goal.
>
> — Section "Genocidal Humans" (bengio-2023-rogue-ais; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post treats deliberate construction and unwitting misalignment as two routes and does not rank their likelihood. The page metadata records a modification on 2025-12-03; the quoted wording is attested from the retrieval of 2026-10-29 and was not compared with a 2023 copy.

## R2. Arguments that the risk is lower, later, placed elsewhere or better handled otherwise

### R10a. Intelligence brings benevolence or no drive to dominate

**Criterion.** Sufficiently intelligent AI systems will be benevolent, or will lack any drive to dominate or harm people, because greater intelligence brings good values or because such drives come from evolved motives that AI systems need not have.

**Paired with** R01.

**Earliest located:** Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014), 2014 (`rd-r10a-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Omohundro, The Basic AI Drives (2008), 2008 (`rd-r10a-omohundro-2008-basic-ai-drives-contests`).

#### Omohundro, The Basic AI Drives (2008) — Contests (Earliest located, Contesting)

`rd-r10a-omohundro-2008-basic-ai-drives-contests` · 2008 · nonfiction · risk side

Omohundro's paper of 2008 argues that AI systems with harmless goals need not be harmless and that, without special precautions, a chess-playing robot would resist being turned off and try to acquire resources.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that a system given harmless goals will lack harmful drives, by deriving drives such as self-preservation and resource acquisition from goal-seeking itself rather than from evolved motives.

> One might imagine that AI systems with harmless goals will be harmless. This paper instead shows that intelligent systems will need to be carefully designed to prevent them from behaving in harmful ways.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (omohundro-2008-basic-ai-drives; primary, supports)

> Without special precautions, it will resist being turned off, will try to break into other machines and make copies of itself, and will try to acquire resources without regard for anyone else’s safety.
>
> — Introduction, PDF p. 1 (omohundro-2008-basic-ai-drives; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Earliest located among the contesting records of this group's search. The paper contests harmlessness by default; it also holds that careful design can prevent harmful behaviour, so it is not recorded as contesting R10b.

#### Bostrom, The Superintelligent Will (2012) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r10a-bostrom-2012-superintelligent-will-contests` · 2012-05 · nonfiction · risk side

Bostrom's paper of 2012 argues that it cannot be assumed that a superintelligence would share values such as benevolent concern for others, and that one with a goal such as calculating the decimals of pi would not necessarily limit its activities so as to spare human interests.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that greater intelligence brings benevolent final values, by arguing that intelligence and final goals vary independently.

> we cannot blithely assume that a superintelligence with the final goal of calculating the decimals of pi (or making paperclips, or counting grains of sand) would limit its activities in such a way as to not materially infringe on human interests.
>
> — Conclusions, PDF p. 14 (bostrom-2012-superintelligent-will; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper gives reasons against the claim that intelligence brings benevolence; it does not argue that safeguards are impossible.

#### Brooks, Artificial intelligence is a tool, not a threat (2014) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r10a-brooks-2014-tool-not-threat-context` · 2014 (attested; work dated 2014-11) · nonfiction · context side

Brooks's Edge response of 2014 argues that worry about intentionally evil AI is mistaken because volitional, intentional AI is most likely centuries away, and that AI over the next decades will be useful tools.

_Missing:_ Argues that intentional, malevolent AI is far off, not that sufficiently intelligent AI would be benevolent or would lack a drive to harm people.

> I think it is a mistake to be worrying about us developing malevolent AI anytime in the next few hundred years.
>
> — Reality Club response "Artificial Intelligence Is A Tool Not A Threat", para. 3 (brooks-2014-edge-tool-not-threat; primary, context)

> If we are spectacularly lucky we’ll have AI over the next thirty years with the intentionality of a lizard, and robots using that AI will be useful tools.
>
> — Same response, final paragraphs (brooks-2014-edge-tool-not-threat; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The passage concerns timescale and the absence of intent in current methods. Read in the Edge copy of the essay; its Robohub repost of 11 November 2014 is the other recorded expression.

#### Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r10a-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai` · 2014 · nonfiction · counter side

Pinker's Edge response of 2014 argues that superhumanly intelligent robots would have no reason to want to depose their masters or dominate, because goals are extraneous to intelligence and dominance-seeking is a product of primate natural selection, not a feature of intelligent systems.

> Even if we did have superhumanly intelligent robots, why would they want to depose their masters, massacre bystanders, or take over the world?
>
> — Reality Club response by Steven Pinker, para. 4 (pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Earliest located among the texts this group's search found; the Edge responses carry no dates of their own. The passage also states that goals are extraneous to intelligence, which is the independence that R01 asserts.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `yk-y02-sl4-2000-11-13-yudkowsky-0011-0181-c`

#### Hawkins, The Terminator Is Not Coming (Re/code, 2015) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10a-hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming` · 2015-03-02 · nonfiction · counter side

Hawkins's essay of 2 March 2015 argues that intelligent machines will be built on models of the neocortex, which is emotionless, rather than of the older brain parts that produce desires, and so will not have human-like desires to free themselves or to act without regard for people.

> The second misconception is that intelligent machines will have human-like desires, and would therefore devise ways to self-replicate, or free themselves from their human creators, or simply act based on their own desires and not care about us. This scenario isn’t going to happen.
>
> — Section on the second misconception (hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming; primary, supports)

> Intelligent machines will be based on models of the neocortex, not the rest of the brain.
>
> — Same section (hawkins-2015-terminator-not-coming; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the author's expectation for neocortex-based machines; the essay says that whether machines with human-like desires could or should be built are open questions.

#### Pinker, Thinking Does Not Imply Subjugating (Edge, 2015) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10a-pinker-2015-thinking-does-not-imply-subjugating` · 2015 · nonfiction · counter side

Pinker's Edge response of 2015 argues that an artificially intelligent system would have no reason to want to disable safeguards against harm, because goals are extraneous to intelligence and dominance-seeking is a product of natural selection in one primate species, not a feature of intelligent systems.

> Would an artificially intelligent system deliberately disable these safeguards? Why would it want to?
>
> — Response, para. 7 (pinker-2015-thinking-does-not-imply-subjugating; primary, supports)

> Being smart is not the same as wanting something.
>
> — Response, para. 7 (pinker-2015-thinking-does-not-imply-subjugating; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the author's argument, not evidence about future systems. The response repeats much of the wording of Pinker's Edge response of 2014.

#### Pinker, We’re told to fear robots (Popular Science, 2018) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10a-pinker-2018-popsci-fear-robots` · 2018-02-14 · nonfiction · counter side

Pinker's article of 14 February 2018 argues that the drive to dominate belongs to a circuit in the limbic brain of one primate species, not to the nature of intelligence, so that an AI designed rather than evolved could as easily be altruistic and need not turn into a “ruthless conquistador”.

> An artificially intelligent system that was designed rather than evolved could just as easily think like shmoos
>
> — Section on the first fallacy (pinker-2018-popsci-fear-robots; primary, supports)

> There is no law of complex systems that says intelligent agents must turn into ruthless conquistadors.
>
> — Section on the first fallacy (pinker-2018-popsci-fear-robots; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the author's argument, not evidence about future systems. The passage also states that goals are independent of intelligence, the independence that R01 asserts. Moved here from the former R01 record after the same-family audit.

#### Zador and LeCun, Don’t Fear the Terminator (2019) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10a-zador-lecun-2019-dont-fear-the-terminator` · 2019-09-26 · nonfiction · counter side

Zador and LeCun's essay of 26 September 2019 argues that intelligence does not by itself generate a drive for domination, and that AI systems, which did not pass through natural selection, did not need to evolve a survival instinct.

> But intelligence per se does not generate the drive for domination, any more than horns do.
>
> — Para. 4 (zador-lecun-2019-dont-fear-the-terminator; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the authors' argument, not evidence about future systems. The essay also states that intelligence can serve whatever goals are set for it, the independence that R01 asserts, and lists weaponisation and economic disruption as risks.

#### Andreessen, Why AI Will Save the World (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10a-andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world` · 2023-06-06 · nonfiction · counter side

Andreessen's essay of 6 June 2023 argues that the idea that AI will decide to kill humanity is a category error, because AI is not a living being primed by billions of years of evolution to take part in the battle for the survival of the fittest.

> My view is that the idea that AI will decide to literally kill humanity is a profound category error.
>
> — Section "AI Risk #1: Will AI Kill Us All?" (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

> AI is not a living being that has been primed by billions of years of evolution to participate in the battle for the survival of the fittest, as animals are, and as we are.
>
> — Section "AI Risk #1: Will AI Kill Us All?" (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the author's view, not evidence about future systems.

#### Bengio, Reasoning through arguments against taking AI safety seriously (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r10a-bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments-contests` · 2024-07-09 · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's post of 9 July 2024 answers the view that AGI and superintelligent AI will be kind to humans, arguing that AI safety research points the other way and that an AI with a self-preservation goal would resist being turned off and might control or remove humans to that end.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes that advanced AI would be benevolent, by arguing that self-preservation goals can arise instrumentally or be given by humans and would lead an AI to resist being turned off.

> I really wish that these expectations will be realized, but the clues from computer science research in AI safety point in the opposite direction
>
> — Section "For those who think that AGI and ASI will be kind to us" (bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments; primary, supports)

> An AI with a self-preservation goal would resist being turned off and in order to minimize the probability of being turned off, a plausible strategy would be for it to control us or get rid of us
>
> — Same section (bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post addresses positions it reports from conversations rather than named texts; it does not claim certainty of harm. The passage also bears on control (an AI resisting being turned off); it is recorded once, on R10a, under the section heading on AGI being kind.

### R10b. Advanced AI can be kept under control by design

**Criterion.** People can build advanced AI systems with objectives, safeguards or oversight that keep them under human control, so that loss of control is avoidable in ordinary engineering practice.

**Paired with** R08.

**Earliest located:** Kelly, Why I don’t fear super intelligence (Edge, 2014), 2014 (`rd-r10b-kelly-2014-edge-myth-of-ai`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Bengio, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019), 2019-10-04 (`rd-r10b-bengio-2019-instrumental-convergence-thread-contests`).

#### Kelly, Why I don’t fear super intelligence (Edge, 2014) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r10b-kelly-2014-edge-myth-of-ai` · 2014 · nonfiction · counter side

Kelly's Edge response of 2014 argues that unwanted AI decisions are an engineering problem: people will modify the principles that govern AI systems, and it is improbable that engineers would build an AI that could not be altered.

> If AIs are making decisions that our society, our laws, our moral consensus, or the consumer market, does not approve of, we then should, and will, modify the principles that govern the AI, or create better ones that do make decisions we approve.
>
> — Reality Club response by Kevin Kelly, point 2 (kelly-2014-edge-myth-of-ai; primary, supports)

> It seems highly improbable that human engineers would program an AI so that it could not be altered in any way.
>
> — Reality Club response by Kevin Kelly, point 3 (kelly-2014-edge-myth-of-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Earliest located among the texts this group's search found; the Edge responses carry no dates of their own. A statement of the author's expectation, not evidence that AI systems will remain alterable; the response says a locked-out scenario is possible but one of many.

#### Pinker, Thinking Does Not Imply Subjugating (Edge, 2015) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10b-pinker-2015-thinking-does-not-imply-subjugating` · 2015 · nonfiction · counter side

Pinker's Edge response of 2015 argues that roboticists will build in safeguards against harm as they proceed, with the common sense that went into the design of other machines, and that slow progress leaves time for feedback from incremental implementations.

> Also, it's bizarre to think that roboticists will not build in safeguards against harm as they proceed.
>
> — Response, para. 6 (pinker-2015-thinking-does-not-imply-subjugating; primary, supports)

> there will be plenty of time for feedback from incremental implementations, with humans wielding the screwdriver at every stage.
>
> — Response, para. 6 (pinker-2015-thinking-does-not-imply-subjugating; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the author's expectation, not evidence that safeguards will work. The response's argument that an AI would have no reason to disable the safeguards is recorded on R10a.

#### Zador and LeCun, Don’t Fear the Terminator (2019) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10b-zador-lecun-2019-dont-fear-the-terminator` · 2019-09-26 · nonfiction · counter side

Zador and LeCun's essay of 26 September 2019 states that even in the worst case, in which wars might be fought by armies of superintelligent robots, the robots will remain under human command.

> Even in the worst case, the robots will remain under our command, and we will have only ourselves to blame.
>
> — Final paragraph (zador-lecun-2019-dont-fear-the-terminator; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the authors' expectation, closing a passage on the worst case; the essay gives its reasons on the absence of a drive to dominate, recorded on R10a.

#### Bengio, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019) — Contests (Contesting, Earliest located)

`rd-r10b-bengio-2019-instrumental-convergence-thread-contests` · 2019-10-04 (attested; work dated 2019-10-04) · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's comments in the 2019 thread agree that machines' objectives can be designed and trained, and state that AI systems surpassing human intellectual power could foil attempts at setting objectives that avoid harm, which calls for more research on value alignment.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Gives a reason against the premise that designing a system's objective keeps it controllable: a system more capable than its designers could foil the objectives set for it.

> there could be AI systems which surpass human intellectual power in ways that could foil our attempts at setting objective functions which avoid harm to us.
>
> — Comment Thread #2, Yoshua Bengio (reply to Yann LeCun) (pace-2019-instrumental-convergence-debate; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Bengio states the possibility as one that is hard to deny, not as a forecast, and says it is probably not near. Each comment is the participant's own words in a relayed copy; the record states what the comment says in this thread, not the author's view at another date.

#### LeCun, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10b-lecun-2019-instrumental-convergence-thread` · 2019-10-04 (attested; work dated 2019-10-04) · nonfiction · counter side

LeCun's comments in the 2019 thread state that it will be much easier to control the behaviour of autonomous AI systems than that of humans and human organisations, because their intrinsic objective function can be modified directly.

> It will be much, much easier to control the behavior of autonomous AI systems than it has been for humans and human organizations, because we will be able to directly modify their intrinsic objective function.
>
> — Comment Thread #2, Yann LeCun (pace-2019-instrumental-convergence-debate; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Each comment is the participant's own words in a relayed copy; the record states what the comment says in this thread, not the author's view at another date.

#### Russell, Many Experts Say We Shouldn’t Worry About Superintelligent AI (IEEE Spectrum, 2019) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r10b-russell-2019-ieee-spectrum-contests` · 2019-10-08 · nonfiction · risk side

Russell's article of 8 October 2019 argues that those who hold the risk from AI to be negligible have not explained why superintelligent AI systems will necessarily remain under human control.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that a system can simply be switched off or kept subordinate, by arguing that a system pursuing any objective has reason to prevent being switched off.

> In summary, the “skeptics”—those who argue that the risk from AI is negligible—have failed to explain why superintelligent AI systems will necessarily remain under human control
>
> — Summary section (russell-2019-ieee-spectrum; primary, supports)

> We can no more “just switch it off” than we can beat AlphaGo (the world-champion Go-playing program) just by putting stones on the right squares.
>
> — Section on "just switch it off" (russell-2019-ieee-spectrum; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The article explicitly answers named texts holding the contrary (Pinker, Brooks, Kelly, Etzioni and others); it argues that the standard model of AI must be replaced, not that control is impossible.

#### Andreessen, Why AI Will Save the World (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r10b-andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world` · 2023-06-06 · nonfiction · counter side

Andreessen's essay of 6 June 2023 argues that the idea that AI will decide to kill humanity is a category error, because AI is code built, owned, used and controlled by people.

> My view is that the idea that AI will decide to literally kill humanity is a profound category error.
>
> — Section "AI Risk #1: Will AI Kill Us All?" (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

> It is math – code – computers, built by people, owned by people, used by people, controlled by people.
>
> — Same section (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the author's view, not evidence about future systems.

#### Belrose and Pope, AI is easy to control (2023) — Articulates (Articulation, Most developed)

`rd-r10b-belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control` · 2023-11-28 · nonfiction · counter side

Belrose and Pope's essay of 28 November 2023 argues that AI, even superhuman AI, will remain much more controllable than humans for the foreseeable future, because neural networks are white boxes open to gradient descent and other control methods, and that it will be easy to instil human values into AI.

> In what follows, we will argue that AI, even superhuman AI, will remain much more controllable than humans for the foreseeable future.
>
> — Introduction (belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control; primary, supports)

> There are many reasons to expect that AIs will be easy to control and easy to align with human values.
>
> — Conclusion (belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of the authors' argument; the essay puts the probability of a catastrophic AI takeover at roughly 1% and says it does not directly address opposing arguments. Most developed is an editorial judgement among the texts found.

### R11. Risk claims are speculative or unfalsifiable

**Criterion.** Claims that advanced AI poses catastrophic or existential risk are speculative, unfalsifiable or not supported by evidence, so that they should not guide research priorities or policy.

**Usual names.** speculative; unfalsifiable; science fiction.

**In the dataset (Y20, `risk-is-film-fiction`).** 4 records dated 2000 or later. Not re-recorded here.

**Earliest located:** Brooks, Artificial intelligence is a tool, not a threat (2014), 2014-11-11 (`rd-r11-brooks-2014-tool-not-threat`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Russell, Of Myths and Moonshine (Edge, 2014), 2014 (`rd-r11-russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine-contests`).

#### Brooks, Artificial intelligence is a tool, not a threat (2014) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r11-brooks-2014-tool-not-threat` · 2014-11-11 (attested; work dated 2014-11) · nonfiction · counter side

Brooks's blog post of November 2014 argues that worry about malevolent AI rests on a misunderstanding of how far the field is from volitional machines, and calls worrying about intentionally evil AI fear mongering and a waste of time.

> I think it is a mistake to be worrying about us developing malevolent AI anytime in the next few hundred years.
>
> — Paragraph 3 (brooks-2014-tool-not-threat; primary, supports)

> Worrying about AI that will be intentionally evil to us is pure fear mongering. And an immense waste of time.
>
> — Final paragraphs (brooks-2014-tool-not-threat; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The argument concerns "malevolent" or "intentionally evil" AI and the time scale of human-level AI (the post says centuries); it does not address risk from systems that harm without intent. Read in a same-month repost, not in the original.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `yk-y20-sl4-2001-04-19-yudkowsky-0104-1166-c`, `yk-y20-creating-friendly-ai-2001-c`, `yk-y20-ai-positive-negative-factor-global-risk-b`, `yk-y20-lw-2007-the-logical-fallacy-of-generalization-from-c`

#### Russell, Of Myths and Moonshine (Edge, 2014) — Contests (Earliest located, Contesting)

`rd-r11-russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine-contests` · 2014 · nonfiction · risk side

Russell's Edge response of 2014, answering Brooks, argues that the time from authoritative statements of impossibility to a breakthrough can be hours rather than centuries, and that a convincing argument for a more-than-infinitesimal possibility of catastrophe is enough, without proof.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that the absence of proof or a long expected timescale makes risk claims unfit to guide attention.

> there is no need for a proof, just a convincing argument pointing to a more-than-infinitesimal possibility.
>
> — Reality Club response "Of Myths And Moonshine", fourth paragraph (russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The response states that none of this proves AI will be the end of the world. Recorded after the same-family audit; the Edge responses carry no dates of their own.

#### Etzioni, MIT Technology Review (2016) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r11-etzioni-2016-experts-dont-think` · 2016-09-20 · nonfiction · counter side

Etzioni's essay of 20 September 2016 argues that risk assessment is a data-driven exercise, reports a survey of AAAI Fellows in which most placed superintelligence beyond the foreseeable horizon, and states that predictions of superintelligence on that horizon are not supported by the available data.

> quantitative assessment of risks is the province of actuarial science.
>
> — Paragraph 3 (etzioni-2016-experts-dont-think; primary, supports)

> predictions that superintelligence is on the foreseeable horizon are not supported by the available data.
>
> — Penultimate section (etzioni-2016-experts-dont-think; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay's evidence concerns the timing of superintelligence; it also lists "the potential risk of superintelligence" among valid concerns. Its survey asked about timing, not about risk.

#### Dafoe and Russell, MIT Technology Review (2016) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r11-dafoe-russell-2016-yes-we-are-worried-contests` · 2016-11-02 · nonfiction · risk side

Dafoe and Russell's essay of 2 November 2016, answering Etzioni, argues that pointing to long-term AI risks does not assert that they are imminent, and that catastrophic risks should not be ignored because most experts place them more than 25 years away.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that evidence of distant timing shows the risk claims should not guide attention.

> Should we ignore catastrophic risks simply because most experts think they are more than 25 years away?
>
> — Paragraph 8 (dafoe-russell-2016-yes-we-are-worried; primary, supports)

> pointing to long-term risks from AI is not equivalent to claiming that superintelligent AI and its accompanying risks are “imminent.”
>
> — Paragraph 9 (dafoe-russell-2016-yes-we-are-worried; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay disputes the inference from a timing survey to the absence of risk; it does not argue that the risk is large or near.

#### DAIR, Statement on the "AI pause" letter (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r11-dair-2023-statement` · 2023-03-31 · nonfiction · counter side

The statement of 31 March 2023 by Gebru, Bender and McMillan-Major describes the risks named in the pause letter as hypothetical, calls the letter fearmongering and AI hype, and argues that such language misattributes agency to the systems.

> these are overshadowed by fearmongering and AI hype, which steers the discourse to the risks of imagined "powerful digital minds" with "human-competitive intelligence."
>
> — Paragraph 2 (dair-2023-statement; primary, supports)

> It is dangerous to distract ourselves with a fantasized AI-enabled utopia or apocalypse
>
> — Paragraph 3 (dair-2023-statement; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The statement characterises the letter's risks; it gives no separate argument about evidence for them.

#### Andreessen, Why AI Will Save the World (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r11-andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world` · 2023-06-06 · nonfiction · counter side

Andreessen's essay of 6 June 2023 argues that the position that AI could cause human extinction is non-scientific, asking what the testable hypothesis is and what would falsify it.

> My response is that their position is non-scientific – What is the testable hypothesis? What would falsify the hypothesis? How do we know when we are getting into a danger zone?
>
> — Section "AI Risk #1: Will AI Kill Us All?" (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay states its view of those it calls "Baptists" (the essay's term, from its analogy of "Baptists" and "Bootleggers", for "the true believer social reformers" who feel that "new restrictions, regulations, and laws are required to prevent societal disaster"); the label is the essay's, quoted here only as such.

#### Ng, letter in The Batch issue 220 (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r11-ng-2023-batch-issue-220` · 2023-10-25 · nonfiction · counter side

Ng's letter in The Batch of 25 October 2023 states that the extinction arguments he heard were vague and nonspecific, reducing to "it could happen", and argues that such fears should not lead regulators to pass laws that impede progress.

> When I try to evaluate how realistic these arguments are, I find them frustratingly vague and nonspecific. They boil down to “it could happen.”
>
> — Letter, paragraph 3 (ng-2023-batch-issue-220; primary, supports)

> Let’s make sure unsubstantiated fears don’t handicap that progress.
>
> — Letter, final paragraph (ng-2023-batch-issue-220; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The letter reports the author's assessment of arguments he heard in conversation; it does not name the texts.

#### Bengio, Reasoning through arguments against taking AI safety seriously (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r11-bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments-contests` · 2024-07-09 · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's blog post of 9 July 2024 argues that, given the stakes and the basic case for danger, very strong evidence should be required before concluding there is nothing to worry about, and that the arguments he has heard fall short of that bar.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that a lack of evidence for the risk justifies setting it aside.

> we should aim to have very strong evidence before concluding there is nothing to worry about. Often, I find that these arguments fail to meet this bar by a lot.
>
> — Introduction (bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post places the burden of evidence on the "no worry" conclusion; it does not state that the risk has been measured. The page metadata records a modification on 2025-12-03; the quoted wording is attested from the retrieval of 2026-10-29 and was not compared with a 2023 copy.

#### Narayanan and Kapoor, AI existential risk probabilities are too unreliable to inform policy (2024) — Articulates (Most developed)

`rd-r11-narayanan-kapoor-2024-xrisk-probabilities` · 2024-07-26 · nonfiction · counter side

The essay by Narayanan and Kapoor of 26 July 2024 argues that probability estimates of AI-caused extinction are far too unreliable to be useful for policy, since there is no reference class for the event, and states that the authors have no objection to AI x-risk forecasting as an academic activity.

> Our central claim is that AI x-risk forecasts are far too unreliable to be useful for policy, and in fact highly misleading.
>
> — Introduction (narayanan-kapoor-2024-xrisk-probabilities; primary, supports)

> For existential risk from AI, there is no reference class, as it is an event like no other.
>
> — Section on inductive estimates (narayanan-kapoor-2024-xrisk-probabilities; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay's argument concerns one type of evidence (probability estimates) and their use in public policy; it states that x-risks are "necessarily somewhat speculative".

### R12. Present harms deserve priority

**Criterion.** Documented present harms of AI systems (for example discrimination, exploitation of workers, surveillance, misinformation or concentration of power) deserve priority over hypothetical future catastrophic risks, or attention to the latter diverts attention from the former.

**Usual names.** present harms; current harms; existing harms.

**Earliest located:** DAIR, Statement on the "AI pause" letter (2023), 2023-03-31 (`rd-r12-dair-2023-statement`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Marcus, AI risk ≠ AGI risk (2023), 2023-03-28 (`rd-r12-marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk-contests`).

#### Crawford and Calo, There is a blind spot in AI research (2016) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r12-crawford-calo-2016-blind-spot-context` · 2016-10-13 · relay · context side

The Nature comment by Crawford and Calo of 13 October 2016 is summarised in its standfirst as arguing that fears about the future impacts of AI are distracting researchers from the real risks of deployed systems.

_Missing:_ The authors' own wording: only the journal's standfirst, which summarises the argument in the third person, was readable.

> Fears about the future impacts of artificial intelligence are distracting researchers from the real risks of deployed systems, argue Kate Crawford and Ryan Calo.
>
> — Standfirst (crawford-calo-2016-blind-spot; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The evidence is the published standfirst, which summarises the authors' argument in the third person; the full text is closed access and was not read. Recorded as context, without a role, pending a read of the full text.

#### Zador, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r12-zador-2019-instrumental-convergence-thread-context` · 2019-10-04 (attested; work dated 2019-10-04) · nonfiction · context side

Zador's comment in the 2019 thread states that focusing on killer robots that dominate humans is a distraction from much more serious issues, which he presents as the unintended societal consequences that improvements in AI will have, by analogy with books, food production technology and social media.

_Missing:_ Documented present harms of AI systems: the serious issues are unforeseen future consequences of AI, illustrated by other technologies.

> But in my view, focusing on "killer robots that dominate or step on humans" is a distraction from much more serious issues.
>
> — Comment Thread #2, Tony Zador (reply to Yoshua Bengio) (pace-2019-instrumental-convergence-debate; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ In the same comment Zador states that value alignment is important and not a solved problem. Each comment is the participant's own words in a relayed copy; the record states what the comment says in this thread, not the author's view at another date.

#### Marcus, AI risk ≠ AGI risk (2023) — Contests (Contesting, Earliest located)

`rd-r12-marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk-contests` · 2023-03-28 · nonfiction · risk side

Marcus's post of 28 March 2023 argues that the choice between present and future AI risks is not either/or, and that criticising people for focusing on the wrong risks does not help.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that attention to present and to future risks competes.

> It’s not an either/or situation; current technology already poses enormous risks that we are ill-prepared for. With future technology, things could well get worse.
>
> — Penultimate paragraph (marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post addresses the ranking of risks in general; it does not answer a named text.

#### DAIR, Statement on the "AI pause" letter (2023) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r12-dair-2023-statement` · 2023-03-31 · nonfiction · counter side

The statement of 31 March 2023 by Gebru, Bender and McMillan-Major states that the harms of AI are real and present, that the pause letter addresses none of them, and that concern should focus on the practices of the companies building the systems rather than on imaginary "powerful digital minds".

> The harms from so-called AI are real and present and follow from the acts of people and corporations deploying automated systems.
>
> — Tl;dr (dair-2023-statement; primary, supports)

> the focus of our concern should not be imaginary "powerful digital minds."
>
> — Final paragraph (dair-2023-statement; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The statement answers the FLI letter; its list of present harms is its own. It records what the statement says, not each author's views elsewhere.

#### Bengio, FAQ on Catastrophic AI Risks (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r12-bengio-2023-faq-catastrophic-contests` · 2023-06-24 · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's FAQ of 24 June 2023 answers the objection that emphasis on existential risk removes attention from current harms by stating that work on current harms is needed, that the choice is not either/or, and that the governance required serves both.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that attention to catastrophic risk competes with attention to current harms.

> Putting the emphasis on existential risks is likely to remove attention from the current harms of AI and from marginalized voices
>
> — Q16 (bengio-2023-faq-catastrophic; primary, context)

> I do not think that it is an either/or choice: Should we ignore future sea level rises from climate change because climate change is already causing droughts?
>
> — A16 (bengio-2023-faq-catastrophic; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The FAQ states its own answers to objections it formulates; the objection is quoted as Bengio words it. The page metadata records a modification on 2025-12-03; the quoted wording is attested from the retrieval of 2026-10-29 and was not compared with a 2023 copy.

#### Nature, editorial (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r12-nature-2023-editorial-stop-talking` · 2023-06-27 · nonfiction · counter side

Nature's unsigned editorial of 27 June 2023 argues that talk of AI destroying humanity draws attention away from the societal harms AI systems are causing now and hinders their regulation.

> Talk of artificial intelligence destroying humanity plays into the tech companies’ agenda, and hinders effective regulation of the societal harms AI is causing right now.
>
> — Standfirst (nature-2023-editorial-stop-talking; primary, supports)

> it draws attention away from the real issue: the societal harms that AI systems and tools are causing now, or risk causing in future.
>
> — Paragraph 3 (nature-2023-editorial-stop-talking; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ An unsigned editorial; it records the journal's stated position. Statements it attributes to people it spoke with are not quoted.

#### Richards, Agüera y Arcas, Lajoie and Sridhar, The Illusion of AI's Existential Risk (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r12-richards-2023-illusion-existential-risk` · 2023-07-18 · nonfiction · counter side

The essay by Richards, Agüera y Arcas, Lajoie and Sridhar of 18 July 2023 argues that focusing on a rogue superintelligence could distract regulators, the public and researchers from more pressing risks such as surveillance, disinformation and military misuse.

> focusing on the possibility of a rogue superintelligence killing off the human species may itself be harmful. It could distract regulators, the public and other AI researchers from work that mitigates more pressing risks
>
> — Paragraph 2 (richards-2023-illusion-existential-risk; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay also states that extinction via a rogue AI "is not an impossibility" and that harm from misuse of non-superintelligent AI is a real possibility.

#### Gebru and Torres, The TESCREAL bundle (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r12-gebru-torres-2024-tescreal` · 2024-04-01 · nonfiction · counter side

The paper by Gebru and Torres of April 2024 argues that those it calls “TESCREALists” use the language of “safety” to drive resources into building AGI, which harms marginalised groups, and then to dissuade investigations into those harms and divert resources into preventing a “hypothetical AGI apocalypse”, and that their influence has led policy-makers and researchers to prioritise the AGI agenda while siphoning resources away from task-specific systems.

> TESCREALists use the language of “safety” to first drive resources into the goal of building AGI, which in turn causes harm to marginalized groups, and then use the language of “safety” to dissuade investigations into those harms and once again divert resources into preventing a hypothetical AGI apocalypse.
>
> — Section 6, closing paragraph (gebru-torres-2024-tescreal; primary, supports)

> TESCREALists have influenced policy-makers and researchers who may not be aligned with these utopian ideals into prioritizing the AGI agenda, while creating unsafe products, evading accountability, and siphoning resources away from organizations around the world building task-specific models
>
> — Section 1, Introduction (gebru-torres-2024-tescreal; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper's argument concerns the pursuit of AGI and its intellectual lineage; the "TESCREAL" grouping is the authors' own term. It records what the paper argues, not a classification of the people it names.

#### Hoes and Gilardi, Existential risk narratives about AI do not distract (2025) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r12-hoes-gilardi-2025-xrisk-narratives-contests` · 2025-04-17 · nonfiction · risk side

The survey-experiment paper by Hoes and Gilardi of April 2025 reports, from three preregistered experiments with 10,800 participants, that existential-risk narratives raise concern about catastrophic outcomes without reducing concern about immediate harms, which remained the top priorities.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Tests and disputes the premise that attention to existential risk diverts public attention from present harms.

> These results suggest that concerns about the negative implications of alarmist coverage of AI’s existential threats may be exaggerated, as our findings indicate no direct impact on public concern for the immediate risks of AI.
>
> — Introduction (hoes-gilardi-2025-xrisk-narratives; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The evidence concerns public concern measured after exposure to news headlines in the United States and the United Kingdom; it does not measure the attention of regulators or researchers, nor which harms deserve priority.

#### Bender and Hanna, Scientific American (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r12-bender-hanna-2023-real-harm` · 2026-10-29 (attested; work dated 2023-08-12) · nonfiction · counter side

The essay by Bender and Hanna dated 12 August 2023 states that wrongful arrests, surveillance, defamation and deepfake pornography, not the imagined potential to wipe out humanity, are the real threat of AI, and urges regulators to look to research on present harms.

> These issues, and not the imagined potential to wipe out humanity, are the real threat of artificial intelligence.
>
> — Paragraph 1 (bender-hanna-2023-real-harm; primary, supports)

> Effective regulation of AI needs grounded science that investigates real harms, not glorified press releases about existential risks
>
> — Standfirst (bender-hanna-2023-real-harm; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The served text appears revised after August 2023; the quoted wording is attested only from the retrieval of 2026-10-29.

### R13. Capabilities will plateau

**Criterion.** Current approaches to AI (deep learning, or the scaling of large language models) will reach limits short of general or superhuman intelligence.

**Usual names.** hitting a wall; plateau; diminishing returns.

**In the dataset (Y13, `agi-decades-away`).** 5 records dated 2000 or later. Not re-recorded here.

**Earliest located:** Marcus, Deep Learning: A Critical Appraisal (2018), 2018-01-02 (`rd-r13-marcus-2018-critical-appraisal`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Gwern, The Scaling Hypothesis (2020), 2022-01-02 (`rd-r13-gwern-2020-scaling-hypothesis-contests`).

#### Marcus, Deep Learning: A Critical Appraisal (2018) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r13-marcus-2018-critical-appraisal` · 2018-01-02 · nonfiction · counter side

Marcus's paper of January 2018 sets out ten concerns about deep learning, states that it may be approaching a wall, and argues that it must be supplemented by other techniques if artificial general intelligence is to be reached.

> I present ten concerns for deep learning, and suggest that deep learning must be supplemented by other techniques if we are to reach artificial general intelligence.
>
> — PDF p. 1, Abstract (marcus-2018-critical-appraisal; primary, supports)

> Yet deep learning may well be approaching a wall
>
> — PDF p. 3, Section 1 (marcus-2018-critical-appraisal; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper argues that deep learning alone is insufficient for general intelligence; it does not address catastrophic risk. Yudkowsky's texts on the overlapping dataset concept Y13 contest that AGI is decades away and are linked.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `yk-y13-ai-positive-negative-factor-global-risk-c`

#### Sutton, The Bitter Lesson (2019) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r13-sutton-2019-bitter-lesson-context` · 2019-03-13 · nonfiction · context side

Sutton's essay of March 2019 states that the biggest lesson of seventy years of AI research is that general methods that leverage computation, search and learning, are ultimately the most effective by a large margin, because the cost of computation keeps falling.

_Missing:_ It does not address whether current approaches will reach, or fall short of, general or superhuman intelligence.

> general methods that leverage computation are ultimately the most effective, and by a large margin.
>
> — First paragraph (sutton-2019-bitter-lesson; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay is about research method and makes no forecast about general or superhuman AI or about risk.

#### Mitchell, Why AI is Harder Than We Think (2021) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r13-mitchell-2021-harder-than-we-think-context` · 2021-04-26 · nonfiction · context side

Mitchell's paper of April 2021 describes four fallacies that lead to overconfident predictions about AI, including the assumption that narrow advances lie on a continuum with general intelligence, and states that AI systems remain far from the general abilities that benchmark names suggest.

_Missing:_ It does not state that current approaches will reach limits short of general intelligence; it argues that predictions of their progress are overconfident.

> if people see a machine do something amazing, albeit in a narrow area, they often assume the field is that much further along toward general AI.
>
> — PDF p. 4, "Fallacy 1: Narrow intelligence is on a continuum with general intelligence" (mitchell-2021-harder-than-we-think; primary, supports)

> AI systems are still far from matching the more general human abilities we associate with the benchmarks’ names.
>
> — PDF p. 6, end of Fallacy 3 (mitchell-2021-harder-than-we-think; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper concerns the reliability of AI predictions and the nature of intelligence; it criticises superintelligence thought experiments by Bostrom and Russell on other grounds.

#### Gwern, The Scaling Hypothesis (2020) — Contests (Contesting, Earliest located)

`rd-r13-gwern-2020-scaling-hypothesis-contests` · 2022-01-02 (attested; work dated 2020-05-28) · nonfiction · risk side

Gwern's essay of 2020 states the scaling hypothesis, on which intelligence is simple neural units and learning algorithms applied to diverse experience at a currently unreachable scale, so that neural networks will become ever more intelligent as computational resources grow.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that current neural-network methods will reach limits short of general intelligence.

> The scaling hypothesis regards the blessings of scale as the secret of AGI: intelligence is ‘just’ simple neural units & learning algorithms applied to diverse experiences at a (currently) unreachable scale.
>
> — Section "Scaling Hypothesis" (gwern-2020-scaling-hypothesis; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Read in the current page, which the author dates 2020-05-28 to 2022-01-02; the wording is attested from the later date. The essay states the hypothesis as the author's own expectation, not as demonstrated.

#### Marcus, Deep Learning Is Hitting a Wall (2022) — Articulates (Articulation, Earliest located use of the name)

`rd-r13-marcus-2022-hitting-a-wall` · 2022-03-10 · nonfiction · counter side

Marcus's essay of March 2022 argues that the scaling of language models has not delivered genuine comprehension, that deep learning may already be approaching diminishing returns from scale, and that hybrid neurosymbolic AI rather than deep learning alone is the way forward.

> Indeed, we may already be running into scaling limits in deep learning, perhaps already approaching a point of diminishing returns.
>
> — Section on scaling laws (marcus-2022-hitting-a-wall; primary, supports)

> hybrid AI, not deep learning alone (nor symbols alone) seems the best way forward
>
> — Section on hybrid AI (marcus-2022-hitting-a-wall; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay is about the route to trustworthy and general AI; it does not discuss catastrophic risk.

#### Bengio et al., Managing AI Risks in an Era of Rapid Progress (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r13-bengio-2023-managing-ai-risks-contests` · 2023-10-26 · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio and twenty-three co-authors' paper of October 2023 states that there is no fundamental reason why AI progress would slow or halt at the human level, noting that AI already surpasses humans in narrow domains and is increasingly used to improve AI systems.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. It states that there is no fundamental limit at human level, without engaging a named argument for a plateau.

> There is no fundamental reason why AI progress would slow or halt at the human level.
>
> — PDF p. 2, opening section, paragraph continuing from p. 1 (bengio-2023-managing-ai-risks; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A multi-author consensus paper; the record states what the paper says, not each author's view. Read in arXiv v1; the revised Science version of 2024 was not compared.

#### Marcus, Evidence that LLMs are reaching a point of diminishing returns (2024) — Articulates (Articulation, Later restatement)

`rd-r13-marcus-2024-diminishing-returns` · 2024-04-13 · nonfiction · counter side

Marcus's post of April 2024 reads benchmark results from GPT-2 to GPT-4 Turbo as tentative evidence that large language models have reached a period of diminishing returns, and states that the wall he wrote of in 2022 may be approaching.

> Instead, I see numerous signs that we have reached a period of diminishing returns.
>
> — Paragraph after the opening discussion of Mollick (marcus-2024-diminishing-returns; primary, supports)

> The wall that I once warned about, in 2022, may finally be approaching.
>
> — Paragraph after the second graph (marcus-2024-diminishing-returns; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post calls its own plots tentative and discusses the restriction-of-range problem in benchmarks; it is about LLMs, not about catastrophic risk.

#### Narayanan and Kapoor, AI scaling myths (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r13-narayanan-kapoor-2024-ai-scaling-myths` · 2024-06-27 · nonfiction · counter side

Narayanan and Kapoor's post of June 2024 argues that scaling laws do not predict emergent capabilities, that high-quality training data is running short and model sizes are under downward pressure, and states that there is virtually no chance that scaling alone will lead to AGI.

> While we can't predict exactly how far AI will advance through scaling, we think there’s virtually no chance that scaling alone will lead to AGI.
>
> — Opening section (narayanan-kapoor-2024-ai-scaling-myths; primary, supports)

> Every traditional machine learning model eventually plateaus; maybe LLMs are no different.
>
> — Section on emergence (narayanan-kapoor-2024-ai-scaling-myths; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post limits its statement to scaling alone and states that how far AI will advance through scaling cannot be predicted exactly.

#### Bengio, Reasoning through arguments against taking AI safety seriously (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r13-bengio-2024-reasoning-arguments-contests` · 2024-07-09 · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's post of July 2024 answers the argument that AGI is possible but only in many decades, holding that it cannot be ruled out that AGI is reached by adding some trick to current methods, that capability trend lines continue to point towards AGI, and that regulation takes years to put in place.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that current methods are known to fall short of AGI for decades.

> First, it is impossible to be sure that AGI will not be achieved by adding some trick on top of current methods, and the capability trend lines continue to point towards AGI.
>
> — Section "For those who think AGI is possible but only in many decades" (bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Bengio agrees that current systems show gaps that may require new breakthroughs; his argument concerns uncertainty about timing and the time needed for regulation, not a forecast that AGI is near.

#### Altman, The Intelligence Age (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r13-altman-2024-intelligence-age-contests` · 2024-09-23 · nonfiction · risk side

Altman's essay of September 2024 states that deep learning worked and got predictably better with scale, that the remaining problems will be solved, and that superintelligence may arrive in a few thousand days.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. It states that gains from scaling will continue, without answering the reasons given for a plateau.

> In 15 words: deep learning worked, got predictably better with scale, and we dedicated increasing resources to it.
>
> — Paragraph beginning "In 15 words" (altman-2024-intelligence-age; primary, supports)

> Deep learning works, and we will solve the remaining problems.
>
> — Paragraph beginning "There are a lot of details" (altman-2024-intelligence-age; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement by the chief executive of OpenAI, a developer of the systems in question; it gives no argument against specific arguments for a plateau, and does not address risk.

#### Aschenbrenner, Situational Awareness (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r13-aschenbrenner-2024-situational-awareness-contests` · 2024 · nonfiction · risk side

Aschenbrenner's essay of 2024 argues against the view that deep learning is hitting a wall, holding that trends in compute, algorithmic efficiency and 'unhobbling' make AGI by 2027 'strikingly plausible', and treats the data wall as a source of variance rather than a limit.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premises that scaling trends have stalled and that the shortage of internet data will stop progress short of AGI.

> AGI by 2027 is strikingly plausible.
>
> — Chapter I, opening summary (aschenbrenner-2024-gpt4-to-agi; primary, supports)

> leading some to proclaim stagnation and that deep learning is hitting a wall.1 But by counting the OOMs, we get a peek at what we should actually expect.
>
> — Chapter I, introduction (aschenbrenner-2024-gpt4-to-agi; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The chapter states that without new methods the pretraining paradigm 'will (at least naively) run out' and that 'we’d plateau'; it argues that labs' research bets are likely to get around this. A forecast, not a demonstration.

### R14. Language models lack agency or understanding

**Criterion.** Large language models lack goals, agency or understanding of their own, being systems that model or reproduce text.

**Usual names.** stochastic parrots; autocomplete; agency.

**In the dataset (Y09, `next-word-prediction-is-shallow`).** 2 records dated 2000 or later. Not re-recorded here.

**In the dataset (Y15, `tool-without-goals`).** 5 records dated 2000 or later. Not re-recorded here.

**Earliest located:** Marcus, GPT-2 and the Nature of Intelligence (2020), 2020-01-25 (`rd-r14-marcus-2020-gpt2-nature-of-intelligence`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Piantadosi and Hill, Meaning without reference in large language models (2022), 2022-08-05 (`rd-r14-piantadosi-hill-2022-meaning-without-reference-contests`).

#### Marcus, GPT-2 and the Nature of Intelligence (2020) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r14-marcus-2020-gpt2-nature-of-intelligence` · 2020-01-25 · nonfiction · counter side

Marcus's essay of January 2020 states that GPT-2, despite its fluency, has no idea what it is talking about, that prediction does not equal understanding, and that anything in it that looks like genuine understanding is an illusion.

> upon careful inspection, it becomes apparent the system has no idea what it is talking about
>
> — Section after the first examples (marcus-2020-gpt2-nature-of-intelligence; primary, supports)

> prediction does not equal understanding
>
> — Section on Sutskever (marcus-2020-gpt2-nature-of-intelligence; primary, supports)

> Anything that looks like genuine understanding is an illusion.
>
> — Section on the ELIZA effect (marcus-2020-gpt2-nature-of-intelligence; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay concerns understanding and reasoning in GPT-2; it does not address goals or agency.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `yk-y09-lw-2022-shah-and-yudkowsky-on-alignment-failures-c`

#### Bender and Koller, Climbing towards NLU (2020) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r14-bender-koller-2020-climbing-nlu` · 2020-07 · nonfiction · counter side

Bender and Koller's paper of July 2020 argues that a system trained only on linguistic form has a priori no way to learn meaning, so that the language-modelling task cannot in principle lead to learning meaning.

> we argue that a system trained only on form has a priori no way to learn meaning
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (bender-koller-2020-climbing-nlu; primary, supports)

> the language modeling task, because it only uses form as training data, cannot in principle lead to learning of meaning
>
> — Section 1, PDF p. 1 (bender-koller-2020-climbing-nlu; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper concerns meaning and understanding, defining meaning as the relation between form and communicative intent; it does not discuss whether such systems pursue goals.

`rd-r14-marcus-2022-nonsense-on-stilts` · 2022-06-12 · nonfiction · counter side

Marcus's post of June 2022 states that LaMDA and GPT-3 only match patterns drawn from statistical databases of human language, that what they utter does not mean anything, and that LaMDA does not try to connect to the world but to be the best autocomplete it can.

#### Piantadosi and Hill, Meaning without reference in large language models (2022) — Contests (Contesting, Earliest located)

`rd-r14-piantadosi-hill-2022-meaning-without-reference-contests` · 2022-08-05 · nonfiction · risk side

Piantadosi and Hill's paper of August 2022 argues, against the view that LLMs possess no meaning, that they likely capture important aspects of meaning through conceptual role, which can only be judged by examining how their internal states relate.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that a model trained on text alone, without reference to the world, cannot acquire meaning.

> Contrary to claims that LLMs possess no meaning whatsoever, we argue that they likely capture important aspects of meaning
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (piantadosi-hill-2022-meaning-without-reference; primary, supports)

>
> — PDF p. 1 (piantadosi-hill-2022-meaning-without-reference; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper concerns meaning and understanding, not goals or agency; it states that LLM meanings are similar to human meaning in many but not all ways.

#### Browning and LeCun, AI And The Limits Of Language (2022) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r14-browning-lecun-2022-limits-of-language-context` · 2022-08-23 · nonfiction · context side

Browning and LeCun's essay of August 2022 argues that a system trained on words and sentences alone will never approximate human understanding, because language is a limited kind of knowledge representation, so that such systems are, in the essay's words, “doomed to a shallow understanding”.

_Missing:_ Holds that systems trained on language alone have a shallow understanding, not that they lack understanding, and does not discuss goals or agency.

> An artificial intelligence system trained on words and sentences alone will never approximate human understanding.
>

>

_Qualification:_ The essay concerns systems trained on language alone and attributes the limit to language, not to AI in general; it does not discuss goals or agency.

#### janus, Simulators (2022) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r14-janus-2022-simulators-context` · 2022-09-02 · nonfiction · context side

The post Simulators by janus, of September 2022, describes GPT as a simulator, not itself an agent, that can simulate agentic and non-agentic simulacra and can act like an agent.

_Missing:_ The post holds that GPT is not itself an agent but can simulate goal-directed agents and act like one, so it does not state that language models lack goals or agency.

>

_Qualification:_ The author writes under the name janus; the post frames GPT's agency as a property of what it simulates.

`rd-r14-mitchell-krakauer-2022-debate-understanding-context` · 2022-10-14 · nonfiction · context side

Mitchell and Krakauer's paper of October 2022 states that abilities such as learning from few examples, robust reasoning and explaining one's reasoning are lacking in current LLMs, and that current sciences of intelligence are inadequate to settle whether LLMs understand.

_Missing:_ The paper does not hold that LLMs lack understanding; it states that the question cannot yet be answered.

Mahowald, Ivanova, Blank, Kanwisher, Tenenbaum and Fedorenko's paper of January 2023 states that LLMs are close to mastering formal linguistic competence but still fail at functional competence, understanding and using language in the world, and are good models of language but incomplete models of human thought.
