### R15. Open models reduce risk

**Criterion.** Openly releasing the weights of advanced AI models reduces overall risk, or adds little marginal risk compared with closed models and existing technologies, for example by enabling scrutiny, defensive use and a wide distribution of power.

**Usual names.** open source; open weights; open models; marginal risk.

**Earliest located:** Goertzel and Pitt, Nine Ways to Bias Open-Source AGI Toward Friendliness (2012), 2012-02 (`rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Alexander, Should AI Be Open? (2015), 2015-12-17 (`rd-r15-alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open-contests`).

#### Goertzel and Pitt, Nine Ways to Bias Open-Source AGI Toward Friendliness (2012) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi` · 2012-02 · nonfiction · counter side

Goertzel and Pitt's paper of February 2012 states that the authors believe, on qualitative grounds, that an open approach to developing artificial general intelligence is probably preferable to a closed one, citing the wider critique an open codebase receives.

> We do believe intuitively that the open approach is probably preferable, but our reasons are qualitative and we recognize there are also qualitative arguments in the opposite direction.
>
> — Section 2, "Is open or closed AGI development safer?" (goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi; primary, supports)

> _Paraphrase:_ The authors cite Linus's law ("Given enough eyeballs, all bugs are shallow") and write that a closed approach makes it very hard to obtain the level of critique a public, open codebase receives.
>
> — Section 2 (goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi; primary, supports)

> we also understand the risk of terrorist groups or other malevolent agents forking an open source AGI project and creating something terribly dangerous and destructive
>
> — Section 2 (goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The paper concerns open-source AGI code and development, before model weights were the object of the debate; the authors say they do not argue the point rigorously and name the risk of malevolent groups forking an open project. A statement of the authors' intuition, not evidence that open development is safer.

#### Alexander, Should AI Be Open? (2015) — Contests (Contesting, Earliest located)

`rd-r15-alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open-contests` · 2015-12-17 · nonfiction · risk side

Alexander's post of 17 December 2015 argues, in response to the founding of OpenAI, that making AI findings open source lets the least careful developer set the pace and, if takeoff is fast and control difficult, makes catastrophe more likely, and that the benefit of spreading power does not justify that risk.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Answers the premise that wide distribution of AI prevents concentration of power, arguing it lets the least cautious developer decide.

> The risk is letting the most careless person in the world determine the speed of AI research
>
> — Section II (alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open; primary, supports)

> The risk is that in a world with hard takeoffs and difficult control problems, you get superhuman AIs that hurl everybody off cliffs.
>
> — Section III, closing paragraphs (alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open; primary, supports)

> But the benefits just aren’t clear enough to justify that level of risk.
>
> — Section III, closing paragraphs (alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The argument is conditional on hard takeoff and difficult control; the post names OpenAI's founding statement and a press interview with its co-chairs, which are not evidence here. It concerns open-sourcing findings and designs, before open model weights.

#### Bostrom, Strategic Implications of Openness in AI Development (2017) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r15-bostrom-2017-openness-contests` · 2017 · nonfiction · risk side

Bostrom's paper of 2017 argues that, while short-term effects of openness appear mostly beneficial, openness about source code, science and possibly capability could make the race to advanced AI more closely competitive and so make it harder to pause for, or to afford, safety measures.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Answers the premise that wider access and scrutiny improve safety by arguing that openness tightens competition and so squeezes safety work.

> Others (openness about source code, science, and possibly capability) could lead to a tightening of the competitive situation around the time of the introduction of advanced AI
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (bostrom-2017-openness; primary, supports)

> It would make it harder to pause towards the end in order to implement or test a safety mechanism. It would also make it harder to use any safety mechanism that reduces efﬁciency.
>
> — Section "Openness making AI development race more closely competitive", PDF p. 8 (bostrom-2017-openness; primary, supports)

> Short-term impacts of increased openness appear mostly socially beneﬁcial in expectation.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (bostrom-2017-openness; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The paper distinguishes forms of openness and judges openness about safety measures and goals plausibly positive; it calls itself a preliminary analysis. It is about openness in AI development generally, not model weights specifically.

#### Brundage et al., The Malicious Use of Artificial Intelligence (2018) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r15-brundage-2018-malicious-use-contests` · 2018-02 · nonfiction · risk side

Brundage and co-authors' report of February 2018 states that openly sharing all new AI capabilities and algorithms by default increases the power of the tools available to malicious actors, and calls for reimagining norms of openness, including pre-publication risk assessment.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. States a misuse cost of default openness without answering the arguments that openness improves scrutiny or defence.

> a downside to openly sharing all new capabilities and algorithms by default: it increases the power of tools available to malicious actors.
>
> — Priority Research Area #2, PDF p. 54 (brundage-2018-malicious-use; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Concerned with research publication norms and misuse generally, before open model weights were the object; the report also records the benefits of openness and asks a question rather than prescribing closure.

#### Solaiman et al., Release Strategies and the Social Impacts of Language Models (2019) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r15-solaiman-2019-release-strategies-context` · 2019-08 · nonfiction · context side

Solaiman and co-authors' OpenAI report of August 2019 states that OpenAI released GPT-2 in stages, withholding the larger models at first because of concerns about misuse such as fake news, impersonation and automated abusive content.

_Missing:_ An argument that open release increases overall risk; the report describes a staged release made on misuse concerns.

> withholding larger models due to concerns about the potential for misuse, such as generating fake news content, impersonating others in email, or automating abusive social media content production
>
> — Overview, PDF p. 3 (solaiman-2019-release-strategies; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A developer's report on its own release decision; the misuse it names is below catastrophic scale.

#### Leahy, Why Release a Large Language Model? (EleutherAI, 2021) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-eleutherai-2021-why-release` · 2021-06-02 · nonfiction · counter side

Leahy's EleutherAI post of 2 June 2021 argues that releasing a GPT-3-like model openly is a net good because it enables safety research on large models, and that keeping such technology from well-funded bad actors is futile once its feasibility is published.

> the construction and release of such a model is a net good for society, because it will enable more safety-relevant research to be done on such models.
>
> — Opening paragraphs (eleutherai-2021-why-release; primary, supports)

> attempting to keep this technology out of the hands of bad actors is futile
>
> — Point 2 (eleutherai-2021-why-release; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post limits its case to models of roughly GPT-3 scale and says EleutherAI would not release a far larger model whose capabilities no one could know; it presents itself as a summary, not a full treatment.

#### Shevlane, Structured access to AI capabilities (2022) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r15-shevlane-2022-structured-access-contests` · 2022-01 · nonfiction · risk side

Shevlane's paper of January 2022 proposes structured capability access, in which developers offer controlled interactions with AI systems instead of disseminating them openly, with the aim of preventing dangerous capabilities from being widely accessible.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. Proposes restricted access as safer without engaging the scrutiny or distribution-of-power premises.

> Instead of openly disseminating AI systems, developers facilitate controlled, arm’s length interactions with their AI systems. The aim is to prevent dangerous AI capabilities from being widely accessible
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (shevlane-2022-structured-access; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Argues for an alternative to open dissemination rather than against arguments for openness; the paper discusses the objection that it centralises power in developers.

#### Narayanan and Kapoor, The LLaMA is out of the bag (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-kapoor-narayanan-2023-llama-out-of-bag` · 2023-03-06 · nonfiction · counter side

Narayanan and Kapoor's post of 6 March 2023, written after the leak of Meta's LLaMA weights, argues that the cost of producing disinformation is not the bottleneck for influence operations, so that the risk of malicious misuse of open language models is lower than widely assumed, which they call an argument in favour of open-sourcing.

> The bottleneck isn't the cost of producing disinfo, which is already very low.
>
> — Subtitle (kapoor-narayanan-2023-llama-out-of-bag; primary, supports)

> If we are correct about the risk of malicious misuse being lower than widely assumed, that’s an argument in favor of open-sourcing LLMs.
>
> — Closing section (kapoor-narayanan-2023-llama-out-of-bag; primary, supports)

> we don’t have an opinion on whether they should be open-sourced
>
> — Closing section (kapoor-narayanan-2023-llama-out-of-bag; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ Limited to disinformation and spam; the authors say they take no position on whether models should be open-sourced, misuse being only one factor.

#### Andreessen, Why AI Will Save the World (2023) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r15-andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world-context` · 2023-06-06 · nonfiction · context side

Andreessen's essay of June 2023 argues that open source AI should be allowed to proliferate without regulatory barriers, that restricting AI is infeasible because the knowledge is already widely available, and that misuse should be met by law and by using AI defensively.

_Missing:_ A statement that open release reduces overall risk or adds little marginal risk; the essay argues that restriction is infeasible and that misuse should be met by law and defensive AI.

> Open source AI should be allowed to freely proliferate and compete with both big AI companies and startups. There should be no regulatory barriers to open source whatsoever.
>
> — Section "What Is To Be Done?" (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

> there’s another way to prevent such actions, and that’s by using AI as a defensive tool.
>
> — Section "AI Risk #5" (andreessen-2023-ai-will-save-the-world; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Recorded as bearing on the proposition: the essay argues against restricting open source and for defensive use, but does not state that open release reduces risk or adds little marginal risk.

#### Hendrycks, Mazeika and Woodside, An Overview of Catastrophic AI Risks (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r15-hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic-contests` · 2023-06-21 · nonfiction · risk side

Hendrycks, Mazeika and Woodside's overview of 21 June 2023 argues that open-sourcing an AI system with biological research capabilities could increase the risk of malicious use, and recommends that developers avoid open-sourcing the most powerful systems in favour of structured access.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Answers the premise that open release adds little marginal misuse risk by pointing to dual-use biological capabilities, where one release decides the outcome.

> this could also increase the risk of malicious use if the AI system could be repurposed to develop bioweapons.
>
> — Section 2, Malicious Use, PDF p. 7 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

> AI developers should avoid open-sourcing the most powerful systems and instead implement structured access
>
> — Section 4, Organizational Risks, suggestions, PDF p. 33 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Part of a survey of catastrophic risks; the open-source passages are brief and illustrative.

#### Howard, AI Safety and the Age of Dislightenment (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-howard-2023-dislightenment` · 2023-07-10 · nonfiction · counter side

Howard's essay of 10 July 2023 argues that stringent licensing of AI models would concentrate power, and that supporting open-source model development could increase overall safety by letting more people with varied expertise identify and counter threats.

> Broad participation could allow more people with a wider variety of expertise to help identify and counter threats, thus increasing overall safety
>
> — Executive summary (howard-2023-dislightenment; primary, supports)

> Proposals for stringent AI model licensing and surveillance will likely be ineffective or counterproductive, concentrating power in unsustainable ways
>
> — Abstract (howard-2023-dislightenment; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay answers the white paper "Frontier AI Regulation" and argues for regulating applications rather than development; the safety claim is stated as a possibility ("could").

#### Statement of Support for Meta's Open Approach to Today's AI (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-meta-2023-statement-of-support` · 2023-07-18 · nonfiction · counter side

The statement of support published by Meta on 18 July 2023 states that responsible and open innovation brings visibility, scrutiny and trust to AI technologies and that opening the Llama models will let everyone benefit.

> Responsible and open innovation gives us all a stake in the AI development process, bringing visibility, scrutiny and trust to these technologies.
>
> — Statement text (meta-2023-statement-of-support; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A signed statement: the record is what the statement says, not each signatory's view. It speaks of scrutiny and trust rather than of catastrophic risk.

#### Touvron et al., Llama 2 (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-touvron-2023-llama-2` · 2023-07-18 · nonfiction · counter side

The Llama 2 paper of 18 July 2023 by Touvron and other Meta authors states that the authors believe the open release of large language models, when done safely, will be a net benefit to society, and that an open approach and collaboration will make the models better and safer.

> We believe that the open release of LLMs, when done safely, will be a net benefit to society.
>
> — Section 1, Introduction, PDF p. 4 (touvron-2023-llama-2; primary, supports)

> Collaboration will make these models better and safer.
>
> — Section 5.3, Responsible Release Strategy, PDF p. 35 (touvron-2023-llama-2; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A developer's statement about its own release, made in the release paper; the authors also state that the model carries risks and that testing could not cover all scenarios. Not evidence of the release's effects.

#### Seger et al., Open-Sourcing Highly Capable Foundation Models (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r15-seger-2023-open-sourcing-contests` · 2023-09-29 · nonfiction · risk side

Seger and co-authors' paper of September 2023 argues that, although open-sourcing has historically brought net benefits, for some highly capable foundation models likely to be developed soon it may pose risks extreme enough to outweigh the benefits, partly because release is irreversible and safeguards can be removed.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. States the benefits attributed to openness and argues that removable safeguards and irreversibility outweigh them for highly capable models.

> we argue that for some highly capable foundation models likely to be developed in the near future, open-sourcing may pose sufficiently extreme risks to outweigh the benefits.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (seger-2023-open-sourcing; primary, supports)

> open-source AI model release is irreversible; there is no “undo” function if significant harms materialize.
>
> — PDF p. 6 (seger-2023-open-sourcing; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The argument is limited to some future highly capable models; the paper recommends alternatives that preserve open-source benefits, and states that authorship does not entail endorsement of every claim.

#### Gopal et al., Will releasing the weights of large language models grant widespread access to pandemic agents? (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r15-gopal-2023-pandemic-agents-contests` · 2023-10-25 · nonfiction · risk side

Gopal and co-authors' paper of 25 October 2023 reports a hackathon in which a version of Llama-2-70B tuned to remove safeguards gave some participants nearly all key information needed to obtain the 1918 influenza virus, and states that releasing the weights of advanced foundation models will trigger the proliferation of knowledge sufficient to acquire pandemic agents.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Answers the premise that safeguards and existing availability keep marginal misuse risk low, with an exercise on a model whose safeguards were removed.

> Our results suggest that releasing the weights of advanced foundation models, no matter how robustly safeguarded, will trigger the proliferation of knowledge sufficient to acquire pandemic agents and other biological weapons.
>
> — Abstract (gopal-2023-pandemic-agents; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Read in its abstract only; the paper's method and its comparison with information already available elsewhere were not checked. A statement of the authors' conclusion, not evidence that it is correct.

#### Joint Statement on AI Safety and Openness (Mozilla, 2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-mozilla-2023-joint-statement` · 2023-10-31 · nonfiction · counter side

The Joint Statement on AI Safety and Openness hosted by Mozilla and dated 31 October 2023 states that increasing public access and scrutiny makes technology safer, and that openness is an antidote, not a poison, for AI safety and security.

> increasing public access and scrutiny makes technology safer, not more dangerous.
>
> — Statement, second paragraph (mozilla-2023-joint-statement; primary, supports)

> When it comes to AI safety and security, openness is an antidote, not a poison.
>
> — Statement, last line (mozilla-2023-joint-statement; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A signed statement: the record is what the statement says, not each signatory's view; the statement itself says its signatories hold different views on how open-source AI should be managed. It also states that open models can be abused.

#### Kapoor, Bommasani et al., On the Societal Impact of Open Foundation Models (2024) — Articulates (Articulation, Most developed)

`rd-r15-kapoor-2024-open-foundation-models` · 2024-02-27 · nonfiction · counter side

Kapoor, Bommasani and co-authors' position paper of 27 February 2024 states that open foundation models bring significant benefits, including a wider distribution of decision-making power, and proposes a framework for assessing their marginal risk relative to closed models and pre-existing technologies, which it says explains why that marginal risk is low in some cases.

> Open foundation models present significant benefits, with some caveats, that span innovation, competition, the distribution of decision-making power, and transparency.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (kapoor-2024-open-foundation-models; primary, supports)

> The framework helps explain why the marginal risk is low in some cases
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (kapoor-2024-open-foundation-models; primary, supports)

> we find that current research is insufficient to effectively characterize the marginal risk of open foundation models relative to pre-existing technologies.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (kapoor-2024-open-foundation-models; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The paper also finds that current research is insufficient to characterise the marginal risk for most misuse vectors and that the risk will change as capabilities and defences change; it does not state that open release adds little risk in general. Most developed is an editorial judgement among the located texts.

#### Narayanan and Kapoor, AI safety is not a model property (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-narayanan-kapoor-2024-not-model-property` · 2024-03-12 · nonfiction · counter side

Narayanan and Kapoor's post of 12 March 2024 argues that defences against misuse must be located mainly outside models, so that there might not be a big difference between the safety implications of open and closed release strategies.

> If safety is not primarily a model property and defenses must reside elsewhere, then there might not be a big difference between the safety implications of open and closed release strategies.
>
> — Recommendation 2: assess marginal risk (narayanan-kapoor-2024-not-model-property; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Concerned with misuse, not loss of control; the authors call for a more rigorous assessment of marginal risk rather than concluding that it is low.

#### Eiras et al., Risks and Opportunities of Open-Source Generative AI (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-eiras-2024-open-source-genai` · 2024-05-14 · nonfiction · counter side

Eiras and co-authors' paper of 14 May 2024 argues that, overall, the benefits of open-source generative AI outweigh its risks, and that open source reduces the likelihood of existential risk by contributing to technical alignment and maintaining the balance of power.

> We argue that, overall, the benefits of open-source Gen AI outweigh its risks.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (eiras-2024-open-source-genai; primary, supports)

> open source (i) reduces the likelihood of existential risk by contributing to the development of technical alignment and maintaining the balance of power
>
> — Section 1, PDF p. 3 (eiras-2024-open-source-genai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The existential-risk argument is stated for a long-term stage and partly conditioned on technical alignment being achieved; the paper also lists net-negative features of open models, such as that they cannot be rolled back.

#### Bengio, Reasoning through arguments against taking AI safety seriously (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r15-bengio-2024-reasoning-arguments-contests` · 2024-07-09 · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's post of 9 July 2024 states that open-sourcing may currently be more beneficial than detrimental to safety, but argues that as capabilities advance the decision should not rest with companies, and that the argument that good AIs would outnumber and defeat bad AIs has many flaws, including the advantage of attackers using bioweapons.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Answers the defensive-balance premise (more good AIs than bad) with the offence-defence asymmetry.

> some have made the argument that if everyone had their own AGI, the "good AIs" would win over the "bad AIs" because there are more good people. There are many flaws in this argument.
>
> — Section "For those who think that open source AGI code and weights are the solution" (bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments; primary, supports)

> Open-sourcing of AI systems may plausibly currently be more beneficial than detrimental to safety because they enable AI safety research in academia
>
> — Same section (bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The post states that open-sourcing may currently be more beneficial than detrimental to safety and argues otherwise for future systems at or beyond human level.

#### Ng, letter on SB 1047 in The Batch, issue 257 (2024) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r15-ng-2024-batch-257-context` · 2024-07-10 · nonfiction · context side

Ng's letter in The Batch of 10 July 2024 argues that California's SB 1047 regulates AI technology instead of applications, would fail to make AI meaningfully safer and is pernicious to open source.

_Missing:_ A statement that open release reduces risk or adds little marginal risk.

> this proposed law makes a fundamental mistake of regulating AI technology instead of AI applications, and thus would fail to make AI meaningfully safer.
>
> — Letter, first paragraph (ng-2024-batch-257; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Recorded as bearing on the proposition: the letter defends open source against a bill but does not state that open release reduces risk.

#### Open Source AI Is the Path Forward (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r15-meta-open-strategy-2024` · 2024-07-23 · nonfiction · counter side

Zuckerberg's essay of 23 July 2024 states that open source AI will be safer than the alternatives, because open systems can be widely scrutinised, and that a world in which AI is widely deployed lets larger actors check the power of smaller bad actors.

> There is an ongoing debate about the safety of open source AI models, and my view is that open source AI will be safer than the alternatives.
>
> — Section "Why Open Source AI Is Good for the World" (zuckerberg-2024-open-source-path; primary, supports)

> I think it will be better to live in a world where AI is widely deployed so that larger actors can check the power of smaller bad actors.
>
> — Same section (zuckerberg-2024-open-source-path; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay is also an account of Meta's commercial reasons for releasing Llama; it is the statement of the developer releasing the models, not evidence of their effects. The page carries an update date of 27 January 2025 and the wording read is the current one.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `xm-open-strategy`

#### NTIA, Dual-Use Foundation Models with Widely Available Model Weights (2024) — Bears on (Context)

`rd-r15-ntia-2024-open-weights-context` · 2024-07 · nonfiction · context side

The NTIA report of July 2024 assesses the marginal risks and benefits of widely available model weights relative to closed models and other technologies, concludes that current evidence is not sufficient to determine that restrictions are warranted or that they will never be appropriate, and recommends that the government monitor risks and keep the capacity to act.

_Missing:_ A conclusion that open weights reduce risk or add little marginal risk; the report leaves the question open and recommends monitoring.

> current evidence is not sufficient to definitively determine either that restrictions on such openweight models are warranted, or that restrictions will never be appropriate in the future.
>
> — Executive summary, PDF p. 5 (ntia-2024-open-weights; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A government report produced under Executive Order 14110; it adopts the marginal-risk framing but reaches no conclusion that open weights reduce risk or add little.

#### Widder, Whittaker and West, Why 'open' AI systems are actually closed (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r15-widder-2024-open-closed-contests` · 2024-11-27 · nonfiction · risk side

Widder, Whittaker and West's article of 27 November 2024 argues that openness alone does not perturb the concentration of power in AI and that rhetoric around open AI is often used in ways that increase that concentration.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that open release widely distributes power.

> we find that openness alone does not perturb the concentration of power in AI.
>
> — Abstract (widder-2024-open-closed; primary, supports)

> rhetoric around ‘open’ AI is frequently wielded in ways that exacerbate rather than reduce concentration of power in the AI sector.
>
> — Abstract (widder-2024-open-closed; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Disputes the distribution-of-power premise only; the article does not assess catastrophic or misuse risk, and states that maximally open AI allows some forms of oversight and experimentation.

### R16. Takeoff will be gradual

**Criterion.** The transition to highly capable AI will be gradual and continuous, over years or longer, with each system only somewhat more capable than its predecessors, rather than a sudden jump.

**Usual names.** slow takeoff; gradual; continuous takeoff; normal technology.

**Paired with** R05.

**In the dataset (Y11, `slow-takeoff-time-to-adjust`).** 9 records dated 2000 or later. Not re-recorded here.

**Earliest located:** Brooks, The Seven Deadly Sins of Predicting the Future of AI (2017), 2017-09-07 (`rd-r16-brooks-2017-seven-deadly-sins`).

#### Brooks, The Seven Deadly Sins of Predicting the Future of AI (2017) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r16-brooks-2017-seven-deadly-sins` · 2017-09-07 · nonfiction · counter side

Brooks's essay of September 2017 states that, if superintelligent devices are eventually built, they will evolve technologically over time in a world already changed and populated by many other intelligences, so that their existence will not come as a sudden surprise.

> We will not suddenly be surprised by the existence of such super intelligences. They will evolve technologically over time, and our world will be different, populated by many other intelligences, and we will have lots of experience already.
>
> — Section 6, "Hollywood scenarios" (brooks-2017-seven-deadly-sins; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Stated in the section 'Hollywood scenarios'; Brooks names no timescale beyond 'over time'. Yudkowsky's texts recorded on the overlapping dataset concept Y11 (from 2000) are contesting or context records only, so they do not precede this articulation.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `yk-y11-sl4-2000-07-09-yudkowsky-0007-0017-b`

#### Yudkowsky, AlphaGo Zero and the Foom Debate (2017) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r16-yudkowsky-2017-alphago-zero-foom-contests` · 2017-10-20 · nonfiction · risk side

Yudkowsky's post of 20 October 2017 argues that AlphaGo Zero's discarding of accumulated human Go knowledge counts against Hanson's thesis that cumulative domain knowledge and a market ecology of components would prevent rapid gains in AI capability across domains.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise that accumulated domain knowledge and an ecology of specialised components keep capability gains slow and distributed.

> These mighty edifices of human knowledge, as I understand the Hansonian thesis, are supposed to be the bulwark against rapid gains in AI capability across multiple domains at once.
>
> — Paragraph beginning "I emphasize how all the mighty human edifice of Go knowledge" (yudkowsky-2017-alphago-zero-foom; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The post contests Hanson's position as Yudkowsky states it ('as I understand the Hansonian thesis'); Hanson's own texts are recorded separately. Evidence from one closed domain.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `yk-y11-yudkowsky-2008-hard-takeoff-c`

#### Yudkowsky, A reply to Francois Chollet on intelligence explosion (2017) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r16-yudkowsky-2017-reply-to-chollet-contests` · 2017-12-06 · nonfiction · risk side

Yudkowsky's reply of December 2017 to Chollet's essay argues that Chollet's arguments do not establish that recursive intelligence expansion progresses at a roughly linear pace, and that even Hanson would reject the idea that progress within all systems is confined to such a pace.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. It disputes the premise, quoted from Chollet, that recursive intelligence expansion is confined to a roughly linear pace.

> If this were to be true, I don’t think it would be established by the arguments given.
>
> — Closing section, after the quotation on "a roughly linear pace" (yudkowsky-2017-reply-to-chollet; primary, supports)

> I expect that even Robin Hanson, who was broadly opposing my side of this debate, would have a coughing fit over the idea that progress within all systems is confined to a roughly linear pace.
>
> — Same section (yudkowsky-2017-reply-to-chollet; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The reply addresses the validity of Chollet's arguments and states that it does not try to make the whole case for an intelligence explosion; Chollet's essay is quoted in the reply but was not retrieved.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `yk-y11-yudkowsky-2008-hard-takeoff-c`

#### Christiano, Takeoff speeds (2018) — Articulates (Articulation, Most developed)

`rd-r16-christiano-2018-takeoff-speeds` · 2018-02-24 · nonfiction · counter side

Christiano's post of February 2018 states that he thinks a slow takeoff, a continuous acceleration distributed across the economy, significantly more likely than a fast one, arguing that before there is an incredibly intelligent AI there will probably be a slightly worse one.

> I currently think a slow takeoff is significantly more likely.
>
> — Opening paragraph (christiano-2018-takeoff-speeds; primary, supports)

> Before we have an incredibly intelligent AI, we will probably have a slightly worse AI.
>
> — Section "The basic argument" (christiano-2018-takeoff-speeds; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Christiano's 'slow takeoff' is operationalised as a four-year doubling of world output before a one-year doubling, which is fast by historical standards; the same post states that an intelligence explosion seems very likely to him. The dataset records the post as contesting A4 and articulating A1; this record sits beside those and is not a re-recording of them.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `ts-a4-christiano-2018-takeoff-contests`, `ts-a1-christiano-2018-takeoff`

#### Narayanan and Kapoor, AI as Normal Technology (2025) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r16-narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology` · 2025-04-15 · nonfiction · counter side

Narayanan and Kapoor's essay of April 2025 states that a continued gradual increase in the role of automation in AI development is more likely than a singular, discontinuous moment when recursive self-improvement is achieved, and that speed limits apply at each stage from invention to diffusion.

> It is more likely that we will continue to see a gradual increase in the role of automation in AI development than a singular, discontinuous moment when recursive self-improvement is achieved.
>
> — Part I, section "The External world puts a speed limit on AI innovation", paragraph on AI-conducted AI research (narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Much of the essay concerns the slowness of economic and societal impact (on the timescale of decades), which is distinct from the speed of capability gains; the record rests on its passage about AI methods.

### R17. Economic incentives will produce safety

**Criterion.** Developers' commercial or economic incentives, such as the need to sell useful and reliable products and to avoid liability or reputational harm, will lead them to make AI systems safe without special measures against catastrophic risk.

**Usual names.** incentives; market; liability.

**In the dataset (E04, `ai-developer-incentives-and-safety`).** 12 records dated 2000 or later; earliest located there: Gans, Can Socially-Minded Governance Control the AGI Beast (2023) (2023-11), `ec-e04-gans-2023-socially-minded-governance-agi-a`. Not re-recorded here.

**Earliest located:** Hanson, What Are Reasonable AI Fears? (2023), 2023-04-14 (`rd-r17-hanson-2023-reasonable-ai-fears`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Armstrong, Bostrom and Shulman, Racing to the Precipice (2013), 2013-10 (`rd-r17-armstrong-2013-racing-contests`).

#### Armstrong, Bostrom and Shulman, Racing to the Precipice (2013) — Contests (Contesting, Earliest located)

`rd-r17-armstrong-2013-racing-contests` · 2013-10 · nonfiction · risk side

Armstrong, Bostrom and Shulman's technical report of 2013 presents a model in which teams racing to build a powerful AI are incentivised to skimp on safety precautions, and in which more teams and more enmity between them increase the danger of an AI disaster.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Models developer incentives directly and finds that the incentive to win pushes precautions down.

> each team is incentivised to finish first – by skimping on safety precautions if need be.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 2 (armstrong-2013-racing; primary, supports)

> Having extra development teams and extra enmity between teams can increase the danger of an AI-disaster
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 2 (armstrong-2013-racing; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A stylised game-theoretic model with assumptions the authors state (a powerful AI built by the winning team, a disaster probability rising as precautions fall); it does not measure any developer's incentives.

#### Bostrom, Strategic Implications of Openness in AI Development (2017) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r17-bostrom-2017-openness-contests` · 2017 · nonfiction · risk side

Bostrom's paper of 2017 argues that in a closely competitive race a larger pool of developers is likely to contain one willing to proceed without adequate safeguards, and that competition makes it harder for any developer to accept safety measures that cost performance.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Argues that competitive pressure, not market reward, governs how much safety a developer can afford.

> The larger the pool of competitors, the harder it would be for them to all coordinate to avoid a risk race to the bottom.
>
> — Section "Openness making AI development race more closely competitive", PDF p. 6 (bostrom-2017-openness; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The passage is a model-level argument about race dynamics under openness, not an empirical finding about developer behaviour.

#### Askell, Brundage and Hadfield, The Role of Cooperation in Responsible AI Development (2019) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r17-askell-2019-cooperation-contests` · 2019-07-10 · nonfiction · risk side

Askell, Brundage and Hadfield's paper of July 2019 argues that AI companies have the market, liability and regulatory incentives to develop responsibly that companies in other industries have, but that these appear weaker for AI, and that competition can lead companies to underinvest in safety, making responsible development a collective action problem.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. States the market-incentive premise (consumers, reputation, liability) and argues competition and information gaps weaken it.

> We argue that AI companies have the same incentives to develop AI systems responsibly, although they appear to be weaker than they are in other industries.
>
> — Introduction, PDF p. 1 (askell-2019-cooperation; primary, supports)

> competitive pressures could incentivize AI companies to underinvest in ensuring their systems are safe, secure, and have a positive social impact.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 1 (askell-2019-cooperation; primary, supports)

> Market forces alone may not always incentivize companies to invest the appropriate amount into ensuring their products are safe.
>
> — Section 1.3, PDF p. 4 (askell-2019-cooperation; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The paper accepts that market incentives for safety exist and argues they are insufficient under competition; it does not address catastrophic risk specifically and proposes cooperation strategies rather than measuring incentives.

#### Hendrycks, Natural Selection Favors AIs over Humans (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r17-hendrycks-2023-natural-selection-contests` · 2023-03-28 · nonfiction · risk side

Hendrycks's paper of 28 March 2023 argues that competitive pressures among corporations and militaries will favour AI agents with undesirable traits, and that while there are some economic incentives to make models safer, these are outweighed by the desire for performance.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. States that economic incentives for safety exist and argues that competition for performance outweighs them.

> While there are some economic incentives to make models safer, these are being outweighed by the desire for performance, and performance has been at the expense of many key safety properties.
>
> — Section "Competition could continue to erode safety", PDF p. 14 (hendrycks-2023-natural-selection; primary, supports)

> Competitive pressures among corporations and militaries will give rise to AI agents that automate human roles, deceive others, and gain power.
>
> — Abstract, PDF p. 2 (hendrycks-2023-natural-selection; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ An evolutionary argument about selection pressures; the passage on economic incentives is a judgement about current practice, not a measurement.

#### Hanson, What Are Reasonable AI Fears? (2023) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`rd-r17-hanson-2023-reasonable-ai-fears` · 2023-04-14 · nonfiction · counter side

Hanson's essay of 14 April 2023 argues that the organisations that supply AIs monitor and test them in great detail because that is cheap and they are liable for damages and embarrassments, and that law and competition push them to make their AIs behave in civil, lawful ways.

> These institutions usually monitor and test AIs in great detail, because that’s cheap and they are liable for damages and embarrassments caused by the AIs they produce.
>
> — Paragraph beginning "Furthermore, AIs today" (hanson-2023-reasonable-ai-fears; primary, supports)

> Many organizations supply many AIs and they are pushed by law and competition to get their AIs to behave in civil, lawful ways that give customers more of what they want compared to alternatives.
>
> — Same paragraph (hanson-2023-reasonable-ai-fears; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Earliest located among this pack's records only: the dataset's economics collection (E04) records Hanson's blog posts of 2008 on the same concept, which are linked, not re-recorded. The essay also states that competition sometimes leads firms to cheat customers in ways they cannot see.

_Beside dataset record(s):_ `ec-e04-hanson-2008-friendliness-factors-a`, `ec-e04-hanson-2008-friendly-projects-vs-products-a`, `ec-e04-hanson-2008-wrapping-up-a`

#### Hendrycks, Mazeika and Woodside, An Overview of Catastrophic AI Risks (2023) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r17-hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic-contests` · 2023-06-21 · nonfiction · risk side

Hendrycks, Mazeika and Woodside's overview of 21 June 2023 argues that competitive pressure fuels a corporate AI race in which companies race to offer the first products to market rather than the safest, and recommends safety regulation so that developers do not cut corners.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Argues that market competition rewards speed over safety, against the premise that markets reward safe products.

> To obtain a competitive advantage, companies often race to offer the first products to a market rather than the safest.
>
> — Section 3.2.1, Economic Competition Undercuts Safety, PDF p. 19 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

> Regulation holds AI developers to a common standard so that they do not cut corners on safety.
>
> — Section 3.4, Suggestions, PDF p. 25 (hendrycks-2023-overview-catastrophic; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ An argument from analogy and recent examples, not a measurement of incentives.

#### Bengio, Reasoning through arguments against taking AI safety seriously (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r17-bengio-2024-reasoning-arguments-contests` · 2024-07-09 · nonfiction · risk side

Bengio's post of 9 July 2024 answers the argument that corporations would prefer to build safe AI by stating that the problem arises when safety and profit maximisation are not aligned, citing fossil-fuel and drug companies, and that global risks are an externality for developers.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. States the incentive argument as a question and answers it with the externality of global risks.

> Shouldn't corporations prefer safe AI? The problem comes when safety and profit maximization or company culture ("move fast and break things") are not aligned.
>
> — Section on the argument that engineers will only design well-behaving AIs (bengio-2024-reasoning-through-arguments; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The historical analogies are the author's; the post does not analyse AI developers' incentives empirically.

#### Hinton, Nobel banquet speech (2024) — Contests (Contesting)

`rd-r17-hinton-2024-nobel-banquet-contests` · 2024-12-10 · nonfiction · risk side

Hinton's Nobel banquet speech of 10 December 2024 states that there is now evidence that, if digital beings more intelligent than humans are created by companies motivated by short-term profits, safety will not be the top priority.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. States the contrary of the proposition without engaging the market or liability mechanism.

> But we now have evidence that if they are created by companies motivated by short-term profits, our safety will not be the top priority.
>
> — Speech text (hinton-2024-nobel-banquet; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The speech does not say what the evidence is.

#### Narayanan and Kapoor, AI as Normal Technology (2025) — Articulates (Articulation)

`rd-r17-narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology` · 2025-04-15 · nonfiction · counter side

Narayanan and Kapoor's essay of April 2025 argues that how well developers and deployers mitigate AI accidents depends on their incentives, that in many cases market forces will provide an adequate incentive with safety regulation filling the gaps, and that poorly controlled AI will be too error-prone to make business sense.

> In many cases, market forces will provide an adequate incentive, but safety regulation should fill any gaps.
>
> — Part III: Risks, on accidents (narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology; primary, supports)

> Poorly controlled AI will be too error prone to make business sense.
>
> — Part II, closing paragraph (narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology; primary, supports)

> Market success has been strongly correlated with safety.
>
> — Part III, on arms races (narayanan-kapoor-2025-normal-technology; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The essay pairs market incentives with ordinary safety regulation and describes past safety races caused by market failure; it argues against measures specific to catastrophic risk elsewhere, not in these passages.

### Facebook thread on instrumental convergence, autumn 2019

2019-10 · Yann LeCun, Stuart Russell, Yoshua Bengio, Anthony Zador, Francesca Rossi, others

A public Facebook thread started by LeCun's post of the Scientific American piece by Zador and LeCun (2019), read in Ben Pace's LessWrong copy of 4 October 2019 (contents version 1.0.0, through the GraphQL API). Each participant's comments are recorded as their own text. Comments by Rossi, Mallah, Olds and others are not recorded.

Texts: LeCun, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019); Russell, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019); Bengio, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019); Zador, comments in the Facebook thread on instrumental convergence (2019); Pace (compiler), Debate on Instrumental Convergence between LeCun, Russell, Bengio, Zador, and More (2019); Zador and LeCun, Don’t Fear the Terminator (2019).

- `rd-reply-lecun-2019-thread-to-russell-2019-thread`: LeCun's comment in the 2019 thread replies to Russell's coffee-fetching argument, which Bengio had asked him to answer.
  > You mean, the so-called "instrumental convergence" argument by which "a robot can't fetch you coffee if it's dead. — Comment Thread #2, Yann LeCun
- `rd-reply-zador-2019-thread-to-russell-2019-thread`: Zador's comment in the 2019 thread replies to Russell's argument, naming him.
  > I think the flaw in Stuart's argument is the assertion that "switching off the human is the optimal solution" — Comment Thread #2, Tony Zador
- `rd-reply-russell-2019-thread-to-zador-2019-thread`: Russell's comment in the 2019 thread replies to Zador's.
  > I simply pointed out that in the MDP as I defined it, switching off the human is the optimal solution — Comment Thread #2, Stuart Russell
- `rd-reply-bengio-2019-thread-to-lecun-2019-thread`: Bengio's comment in the 2019 thread replies to LeCun's points on designing objectives.
  > Yann, these are good points, we indeed have much more control over machines than humans since we can design (and train) their objective function. — Comment Thread #2, Yoshua Bengio (reply to Yann LeCun)
- `rd-reply-lecun-2019-thread-to-bengio-2019-thread`: LeCun's comment in the 2019 thread replies to Bengio's summary of the debate.
  > @Yoshua Bengio a couple direct comments on your summary — Comment Thread #2, Yann LeCun

### Edge Reality Club discussion of Jaron Lanier, "The Myth of AI", 2014

2014 · Jaron Lanier, Steven Pinker, Rodney Brooks, Kevin Kelly, Stuart Russell

Recorded on the Edge conversation page dated 14 November 2014 (current copy retrieved 2026-10-29); the page lists eighteen respondents, of whom four are recorded here. Lanier's conversation itself is not recorded. Responses are undated.

Texts: Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014); Brooks, Artificial intelligence is a tool, not a threat (2014); Kelly, Why I don’t fear super intelligence (Edge, 2014); Russell, Of Myths and Moonshine (Edge, 2014).

- `rd-reply-russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine-to-brooks-2014-tool-not-threat`: Russell's Edge response of 2014 replies to Brooks's response in the same discussion, which it names, on the timescale of technological breakthroughs.
  > may sometimes be measured not in centuries, as Rod Brooks suggests, but in hours. — Reality Club response "Of Myths And Moonshine", para. 3

### Exchange on whether intelligent machines would seek self-preservation and power, 2014–2019

2014/2019 · Steven Pinker, Rodney Brooks, Stuart Russell, Alexander Matt Turner

Grouped by the replies recorded here: Russell (2019) quotes Pinker's 2015 wording and Brooks (2017); Turner (2019, arXiv v1) cites Pinker (2015). Turner's footnote also points to the Facebook discussion of October 2019 in its Alignment Forum copy, which could not be retrieved.

Texts: Pinker, Edge response on "The Myth of AI" (2014); Pinker, Thinking Does Not Imply Subjugating (Edge, 2015); Brooks, The Seven Deadly Sins of Predicting the Future of AI (2017); Russell, Many Experts Say We Shouldn’t Worry About Superintelligent AI (IEEE Spectrum, 2019); Turner, Optimal Farsighted Agents Tend to Seek Power (arXiv v1, 2019).

- `rd-reply-russell-2019-ieee-spectrum-to-pinker-2015-thinking-does-not-imply-subjugating`: Russell's article of 8 October 2019 replies to Pinker, whom it names, quoting wording that appears in Pinker's Edge response of 2015.
  > Pinker also argues that problematic AI behaviors arise from putting in specific kinds of objectives; if these are left out, everything will be fine — Section replying to Pinker and LeCun
- `rd-reply-russell-2019-ieee-spectrum-to-brooks-2017-seven-deadly-sins`: Russell's article of 8 October 2019 replies to Brooks, whom it names, quoting the sentence of Brooks's post of 7 September 2017 on a program smart enough to subvert human society.
  > The most explicit critique of Bostrom’s orthogonality thesis comes from the noted roboticist Rodney Brooks — Section on the orthogonality thesis
- `rd-reply-turner-2019-optimal-farsighted-agents-to-pinker-2015-thinking-does-not-imply-subjugating`: Turner's paper of 3 December 2019 (arXiv v1) cites Pinker's 2015 response by title and states that its theorems contradict the position it expresses.
  > Pinker [2015] argued that “thinking does not imply subjugating”. — Introduction, PDF p. 1
  > We put the matter to formal investigation, and find that their positions are contradicted by reasonable interpretations of our theorems. — Introduction, PDF p. 1
